16,2026-10-04,"If you've looked into building a tiny home in your backyard, you've probably discovered what many have: While they may save you money in the long run, they can be expensive to build.
These homes, called accessory dwelling units, or ADUs, are small housing units ranging from 150 square feet to 1,200 square feet, depending on where you live.
While detached backyard units are popular, ADUs can be incorporated into many parts of a property, including as a garage or basement conversion, an above-garage unit, or an attic or upper-level apartment.
The average cost of a unit can range from $100,000 to $300,000, not to mention the permit cost, and securing a loan to build an ADU can be challenging.
In California, where most backyard homes are being built, ADU building permits cost anywhere from $450 to $15,000 — even before construction starts, Backyard Unlimited, a company that builds ADUs, said.
That's not all: Other costs could include site preparation, which includes anything from inspections to running utility lines. 
To put it another way, if you don't have cash easily available or the ability to access financing for the project, it can be cost-prohibitive, making it harder for middle- and low-income homeowners to actually build one, studies have shown.
To combat this disparity, promote equity, and ultimately propel the construction of much-needed housing, some states — as well as nonprofits in specific cities — have established loan and or grant programs to provide assistance.
The deadlines for these programs vary from state to state. Those interested in building an ADU should be prepared for next year's application cycles, as they often experience high demand.
Here are the ADU programs launched nationwide.
In 2024, Colorado passed HB24-1152 to assist homeowners in building ADUs. The Colorado Department of Local Affairs (DOLA) works directly with local governments as they implement regulatory changes to comply with HB24-1152.
DOLA also runs the Accessory Dwelling Unit Grant Program (ADUG), which provides $5 million to jurisdictions that bring their codes into compliance and support ADUs.
Who can apply? For ADUG, jurisdictions can apply for funding to provide technical assistance to low- and moderate-income homeowners.
The Colorado Housing and Finance Authority (CHFA) is also establishing programs that directly support homeowners.
Total funding available: The state has set aside $5 million for ADUG.
The primary homeowner-directed effort channels $8 million into CHFA's ADU Finance Programs, providing lender support through loans, credit enhancements, and interest-rate buydowns to help homeowners.
When are applications open? Applications for the third round of funding opened on September 28 and will close on Friday, October 23, 2026.
Requirements: Local governments — including those not covered by HB24-1152 — must be certified by DOLA as an ADU Supportive Jurisdiction under the legislation.
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: The Colorado Department of Local Affairs, Colorado Housing and Finance Authority
To help expand the supply of workforce housing, Maui County launched the ʻOhana Assistance program to aid homeowners in building attached or detached ADUs on their properties.
The County of Kaua'i also offers financial assistance for homeowners who build a second unit on their property through the Affordable Additional Rental Unit (ARU) program.
Who can apply? Owner-occupants with qualified residentially zoned property are eligible to apply for both the ʻOhana Assistance program and the Kaua'i Affordable Additional Rental Unit program.
Total funding available: The ʻOhana Assistance Pilot Project provides grants of up to $100,000 for qualifying homeowners.
Kaua'i's ARU program waives certain county development and permitting fees for homeowners who agree to keep the unit affordable to households earning up to 90% of the area median income for at least five years.
Kaua'i's website states that the combined fee waivers and subsidies for an ARU certified through the program can total nearly $20,000.
When are applications open? Applicants should consult Maui's Department of Housing and Human Concerns for the ʻOhana Assistance program. Kaua'i accepts applications year-round.
Requirements:
The Ohana Assistance program: Grant recipients must set ADU rents in line with the county's workforce housing requirements. The ADU must also be rented to full-time residents of Maui County for at least 10 years.
Kaua'i's Affordable Additional Rental Unit program:
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: The County of Maui Department of Housing, Kaua'i County Housing Agency
Under Massachusetts' Affordable Homes Act, homeowners in most parts of the state—excluding Boston—have the right to build ADUs of up to 900 square feet on their property.
To help homeowners build them, Massachusetts has launched a statewide incentive program. The program is in its first phase and focuses solely on property owners.
Who can apply? Any homeowner in Massachusetts living in an area that allows residential ADU construction.
Total funding available:
Massachusetts's ADU Incentive Program pays approved professionals up to $500 to conduct feasibility studies for homeowners. These studies are designed to help property owners make informed decisions about their ADU projects and include:
When are applications open? The program launched in July 2026, and applications remain open.
Requirements:
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: The Massachusetts Housing Partnership, in collaboration with the Executive Office of Housing and Livable Communities
Earlier this year, the Plus One ADU Program reopened and began offering eligible New Yorkers up to $125,000 or $395,000 in financial support and technical assistance to build or convert an ADU on their property. The program is part of Gov. Kathy Hochul's five-year housing plan, which aims to create or preserve 100,000 affordable housing units.
Who can apply? Any municipality in New York State, or any nonprofit housing organization partnering with a municipality, may apply during the open funding round.
Eligible homeowners must earn no more than 120% of the area median income.
Total funding available: In 2026, New York State Homes and Community Renewal rolled out a fourth funding round, allocating the remaining funds authorized under the $85 million, five-year housing plan.
The agency says it has awarded nearly $74 million to 85 municipalities across the state, helping fund the anticipated construction of more than 565 ADUs.
When are applications open? Applications are available through HCR's website and will be reviewed on a rolling basis until all funding has been awarded.
Requirements:
For homeowners:
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: New York State's Homes and Community Renewal
While not exclusively an ADU program, the Vermont Housing Improvement Program funds vacant-unit rehabilitations, ADUs, and new construction.
Through the program, Vermont homeowners can receive up to $50,000 to build an ADU on their property. Both landlords and owner-occupied homes with plans to rent are eligible.
Who can apply? Any Vermont homeowner can apply through one of five regional organizations serving all 14 counties: RuralEdge, Champlain Housing Trust, Cornerstone Housing Partners, Downstreet Housing and Community Development, and Windham and Windsor Housing Trust. These organizations review applications and oversee projects.
Partners anticipate opening an application window later this Fall to assign these funds to projects.
Total funding available: The program operates on Vermont's fiscal year, which runs from July 1 through June 30. For fiscal year 2027, the program received $4 million for incentives and administration.
When are applications open? Applications are expected to open in fall 2026.
Requirements:
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: Agency of Commerce and Community Development
Previously, the state provided $40,000 to Californians for pre-construction ADU costs, including design, permitting, and soil inspections. However, as of 2026, no money had been allocated for the program, a spokesperson with the California Housing Finance Agency told Business Insider.
Who could apply? In the past, Californians who fell within the low- to moderate-income range were eligible.
Total funding available: $100 million was allocated in 2021, and by 2023, it had been fully distributed. The program's funds were supplemented with $25 million in grant funding for the 2023-2024 period, but by 2026, the program had no remaining funds.
When were applications open? The program is no longer accepting applications.
Previous requirements:
Agency or Department in charge of distribution: California Housing Finance Agency

17,2026-10-04,"I've spent six years writing about the workplace, and though this job market has been unexpectedly difficult, I thought I had learned its ins and outs. I've seen readers go through this, and as a freelancer open to a full-time position, I've experienced it, too.
Apply online, post on LinkedIn, get contacted by a recruiter, interview, take a test, interview again— and then get ghosted. Repeat.
When hope began to feel scarce earlier this year, I woke up to an email from a ""recruiter."" His message was long, detailed, and complimentary. He praised my writing and portfolio and explained how closely my experience aligned with a role he was hiring for.
At first, all he asked for in return was my résumé. The email was kind, detailed, convincing — and a scam.
I received this from the recruiter:
""I'm currently partnering with the recruiting team supporting a confidential Senior Editorial Strategy Lead Workplace & Career Content opportunity with Indeed, and I wanted to reach out personally because your profile is unusually aligned with what this team is trying to build.""
His email included a photo of himself, and his signature linked to a LinkedIn profile that he claimed was his. The verified profile was filled with endorsements, an image of himself, and years of experience listed on his page.
I'd heard of recruiter scams before, but I didn't see any harm in his email. I agreed to send him my résumé.
He complimented my work again, gave me a more detailed overview of the role, and laid out the pay range of $125,000-$165,000. I was excited to see the range, especially because it was higher than I had anticipated and more than I had earned in my previous roles.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I thought, He's not asking for anything. Maybe it's real?
After a few days had passed, he told me the hiring team had reviewed my candidacy and, though ""the feedback was positive overall,"" my résumé needed improvement before we could proceed. To move forward, I'd need to have my résumé redone through a service he worked with. Otherwise, he couldn't advance me.
He said:
""My intention is simply to connect you with a resource that can help present your experience at the level the hiring team is looking for. You can then decide for yourself whether the service and investment make sense for you.""
I replied and asked him if there was a fee for the résumé service.
Within the hour, he replied:
""The service does involve a fee. I don't want to quote a specific amount on their behalf because I'm not directly involved in their current pricing structure, and the cost may depend on the level of support and tools you choose.""
They confirmed the recruiter was not affiliated with the company and that the job opportunity was not real.
I received this from Indeed:
""Regarding the outreach you received, it appears to be a fake recruiting email from an impersonated recruiter. Our People team has confirmed that we are not hiring for this position, and that Indeed does not work with outside agencies to fill open roles. We've also found that this email domain has recently been involved in other fake recruiting schemes.""
I stopped replying.
My experience is far from unusual. LinkedIn's 2026 Job Search Safety Pulse found that 36% of recruiters say they've been victims of impersonation, and 67% say job scams are making it harder to build trust with candidates.
How can job seekers tell the difference between a real recruiter and someone pretending to be one? Erica Rivera, a career coach and former recruiter at Indeed and Meta, said these scams can be hard to spot — but there are signs.
If a recruiter reaches out about a job opportunity, applicants should be able to find the position somewhere outside the recruiter's message, ideally on the company's website or a reputable job platform. It can also be a good sign when the recruiter or their colleagues are actively sharing the opening on LinkedIn, she said.
You might also be able to tell by their email address. ""If they have their email included, and it's truly a company email,"" that's another green flag,"" she said. To check, she suggested looking at the company's website for an official email address.
Not every unusual recruiting practice means someone is trying to scam you, but some situations should prompt applicants to verify who they're dealing with.
A recruiter's LinkedIn profile can offer some clues about whether they are who they say they are. Rivera said applicants should be cautious if a recruiter has very little activity or only a few connections. A suspicious-looking LinkedIn profile photo, a newer profile, or a lack of verification can also be reasons to take a closer look.
""I'm always suspicious when I see a recruiter reaching out that isn't endorsed, because if you're going to be reaching out to people, you should validate your profile,"" Rivera said.
Text messages from a recruiter can also warrant some caution. Recruiters are more likely to call a candidate unexpectedly, she said, although texting can happen in some circumstances. For example, recruiters may text someone like a CDL driver because they may be difficult to reach by email.
If a recruiter reaches out via text, Rivera recommends moving the conversation to a phone call and asking them to send the job information by email.
If a recruiter starts asking for sensitive personal information or money, Rivera said applicants should cut off communication. ""Anything tied to Social Security, date of birth, bank account information, marital status, anything like that should be an automatic flag,"" she said.
Recruiters may reasonably ask for a résumé, portfolio, work samples, or other information directly related to the position, but requests that go beyond that should raise immediate concerns.
Being asked to pay for something is another major warning sign. Rivera said legitimate recruiters should not require candidates to pay for résumé services, equipment, or other costs as part of the hiring process. If you're asked to pay for something, stop communicating.
She also recommended reporting the account on the platform the recruiter used to contact you and, when appropriate, reporting suspicious activity to Google. If you're comfortable doing so, Rivera suggested sharing your experience to warn other job seekers.
Even after years of experience, I was still willing to consider a recruiter who appeared to know my work, understood my experience, and seemed to have a legitimate opportunity.
That's exactly what makes these scams so dangerous. They don't always look like scams. Sometimes, they look like the job you've been waiting for.
After not replying to the alleged recruiter for four weeks, I thought I had heard the last from him, but he has since followed up again and asked if I'm still interested in the role.
I won't be replying.
No comments right now, check back later.

My visit to China was many years ago, circa 2009, and I remember quite well that there was far more of a presence of “space stuff” than is generally seen here in the U.S.  Things like astronaut milk ads, or the big space display in the People’s Bookstore in Beijing (the big like 5-story one near the Forbidden Palace and Tiananmen Square [where I was questioned by the authorities about the big package I was carrying, a 12″ Chinese Moon globe I had picked up at the Ancient Observatory]).  I was able to acquire quite a few books while there for my Lunar Library, far more than I normally see at U.S. bookstores (and in the U.S. they’re all about Apollo, Apollo, Apollo, even now all these years later when we should be looking to the future [as the Chinese books do]).
Back in 2011-12, I authored a paper at The Space Review on the concept of a Cislunar Econosphere (a term I coined to highlight the fact that space is 3-D, as well as a sly economics dig at things like the Greater East-Asian Co-Prosperity Sphere).  It was cited by le monde diplomatique, which was cool, LockMart did a Cislunar-1000 study to try to get some money out of NASA, even cribbed my diagram at first, some NASA interns did a study, it was heady times.
But the only folks who seemed to really get what I was talking about were the Chinese, and I could see it in their approach.  Moving amongst Lagrange points and figuring out the future “roads” of cislunar space.  That’s when I decided to stop giving away free ice cream, and I moved to other interests. “Death of ‘drongo’: Are Aussie insults and swearwords dying out?”
I’m glad to report that the art of swearing is alive and well here in Oz, particularly with younger people. No fear of it dying out. Some older words have dropped out of common usage but everybody still knows what they mean. So I could use the expression ‘fair suck of the saveloy’ and Aussies would know what I was saying. Sometimes I am tempted to link to Aussie videos but when I listen to them, have to remind myself that this is still a family-friendly blog after all.
Some older words have dropped out of common usage
That may be true for you, Rev, but my take is a little different. “Australian slang” has long been a bit of a piss-take (pardon my French). There is a long and proud history in Australia of inventing slang neologisms to parody the speech patterns of “everyday Australians” (as if they ever existed). This history goes back at least to 1915, with the publication of  C. J. Dennis’s book  “The Songs of a Sentimental Bloke”. A number of purveyors of these ersatz Australianisms have continued that fine tradition. 
Barry Humphries created  characters such as Bazza McKenzie, and Sir Les Patterson (Minister for the Yartz) to satirise the dubious notion of a larrikin Australian. The tradition has continued with examples such as Paul Hogan’s character Crocodile Dundee, and Steve Irwin’s character ‘Steve Irwin’. Humphreys’ character Dame Edna Everidge looked down on such common folk, although she was much closer to them than she could ever admit.
Most of these improbable grass roots swear-words and -phrases were either invented, or plucked from obscurity and popularized for comic effect.  The words have never really been common parlance. They’re released into the wild as quotes from the original source, propagate in the community for a bit, and then fade away. Occasionally they’ll be resurrected, in a cringeworthy way, by a Prime Minister trying to sell her/himself as a “man of the people” (looking at you Kevin Rudd and John Howard).
Back in Humphreys’ day, most of the Australian audience were in on the joke. And conning gullible outsiders was part of the joke. These days, Australia’s move to ‘multiculturalism’ and globalisation has meant that increasing numbers of the gullible now live here.  (Without wishing to be racist in any way, shape, or form, the name of the ABC piece’s author is Shalailah Medhora. I suspect she’s not from here. And she’s worked for SBS, for goodness sake.)
/rant
As an aside, when I searched for “fair suck of the sav” (not saveloy) in duckduckgo, there were three or four hits from Australia in the first five pages. The rest were serious reports from overseas sources (mostly USian) that weren’t in on the long-running joke. Sucked in, suckers.

To be fair, Alan, it isn’t the voting that’s compulsory; whether you write on the ballot paper, and what you write, is entirely up to you. Popular inscriptions include “none of the above”, and personal advice that is either indecent or physically impossible.
What is compulsory is getting your name checked against the entry in the Electoral Roll (no ID required) and ruled out (literally, with a ruler and a biro)*. There’s also the option of getting a postal vote sent out to you, to be placed in a signed, sealed envelope and mailed back in another (reply paid) envelope.
*The process of checking whether an individual’s name has been marked off more than once is retrospective: it happens after the votes have been counted. Hence the long-standing election advice: “Vote early, vote often”.
On the French student protests mentioned in that France 24 segment, the interview with the teacher representative should be taken with a grain of salt. The student protests are certainly spontaneous, but they are not necessarily driven by the supposed reported demands about class size, conditions, length of the school days, etc. (I believe Aurelien also intimated as much yesterday in the comments section.) This is something the teachers’ unions would have one believe for understandable enough reasons. However, having teenage boys in two different lycées in a 100,000+ pop. French provincial city, I can attest that the demonstrators (or rather protestors) do not really care about their class sizes, etc. — regular (and legitimate) complaints made by teachers — but they are vaguely keen on seeming in solidarity with their peers from the Paris suburbs, an often romanticized topic of French rap which most French teenage boys listen to. Perhaps as a consequence of the latter cultural association, they don’t mind — the surging adolescent testosterone certainly helps — taunting the cops (“les chickens” in current rap-inflected slang) and even some physical conflict. My fear is that now that the re-arming of the police has recently been passed into law, that the Melenchon left, whose major electoral bloc consists of teachers and which is desperate to be in the second round of the forthcoming presidential elections, is actually cynically hoping for “gilet jaune”-style physical casualties among the teenage students, so that it can appear to represent the interests of the protesting high school students and related martyrs to police violence. Of course, if the teenage students were genuinely serious about improving their education, they would demand school vacations be significantly reduced (besides the two months of summer vacation, there is a two-week vacation for every seven weeks of school) and, in exchange for daily school hours being reduced (a legitimate grievance — my sons sometimes go to school from 8 in the morning until 6 in the evening), that there be school on Wednesday afternoon, which there currently isn’t. I don’t see this being something that high school kids are exactly going to lobby for (“we want to go to school more!”). Nor, for that matter, will the teachers unions make this demand, since many teachers are already fairly resentful and feel they are neither paid nor respected enough, so reduced school vacations are not going to fly well. But the recent OECD tests for 15 year-olds revealed that France is pretty much at the bottom in Europe in terms of educational results, particularly for reading comprehension amongst French teenage boys. I seriously doubt though that an overhaul of the French educational system will be made in light of current events, given the political climate and an economic context in which the government would need to spend a lot more money to make things better.

A Supreme Court Battle Over Climate Change Begins (NY Times; archive)
Nearly a decade ago, the outdoorsy, liberal enclave of Boulder, Colo., sued two oil companies over their role in climate change. Now, the Supreme Court will consider whether the groundbreaking lawsuit can ever go to trial.
Its decision will resonate far beyond the Rockies.
Modern society is entirely dependent on fossil fuels; I’m not even sure what the plot is here anymore. What’s the endgame?
A win for Boulder would likely clear the way for dozens of similar lawsuits nationwide aiming to extract billions of dollars from the fossil fuel industry to fight global warming.
But we don’t need that to “fight” global warming. We can already do that today. But that requires very uncomfortable choices.
Which isn’t to say that these companies should get to dictate US energy policy, which they largely do. But even a successful lawsuit, should it be allowed to procedure, 10+ years from now, won’t change that.

News from Japan
I haven’t seen it covered here yet, but there has been a series of high-profile data breaches in Japan, just over the past few months, e.g. personal information held on servers has been compromised at the National Tax Agency, the Digital Agency, Yamato Transport, Sagawa Express, Nikkei, Times Rental Car, Ape House, Shoes Moonstar, Murakami Dot Com, Aflac, Dai-ichi Life, Life Insurance Association, Nichirei, Seicomart, et al.
I have personally been affected by one of these breaches, with a service informing me that an image of my Japanese ID has been compromised. I have been invited to pay a separate service to verify that no fake bank accounts or credit cards have been created. Naturally, there is a lot of outrage about this — why are WE customers being asked to pay? — and a class action lawsuit is apparently being organized.
But there is a larger story brewing here, because by some odd coincidence the Japanese government has been simultaneously pushing for expanded police powers over the Internet, just as all of these “data breaches” have been happening, under the pretense of “active cyber-defense”. Tokyo Shinbun reports that the Police, the Ministry of Defense, and the Jietai (SDF) want expanded police power over every server in Japan:
Measures to Neutralize Cyber Attacks Are Made Mandatory for Businesses and Governments to Report Damage
https://www.tokyo-np.co.jp/article/518787
Under the pretense of “active defense against cyber-attacks”, every single company and ISP would be obligated to give the police back door root access to every server. Of course, with this sort of access, the police could not only shut down any server, but easily monitor all communication through any server in Japan.
I.e., if you control a server, it is not complicated to conduct surveillance via a man-in-the-middle attack (MITM). Normally, all of our emails and HTTPS communication are secured using encryption, but this can easily be defeated by MITM, for purposes of active eavesdropping.
If this law extends to a mandate that ISPs force customers to use a Root Certificate Authority, then even VPN could be defeated via an AITM attack, though it is unclear whether or not this is on the table.
Relatedly, the Japanese government is using the same pretense of “cyber-warfare” to establish a new “Strategic Communication Office” :
 To Establish a “Strategic Communication Office” to Compete With China and Russia
https://www.sankei.com/article/20230124-DPPNMP2V7FLK5KUPWBCKVT5LYI/
…which is effectively a new Ministry of Propaganda.
On social media, people have already started to compare these initiatives to the infamous Peace Preservation Law of the 1920s, and from my vantage point this is not too much of a stretch.
Thanks for this. Your intuition is probably right, imo.

Xiaomi Redmi Android Battery test

Does a market for 4G phones still exist? Xiaomi thinks so, so it released the Redmi Note 17 4G. We’ve already looked at the battery life and charging tests of its 5G sibling and promised to compare the two in a separate article – this is that article. We will, of course, compare it with other models too, but which ones?
We tried to pick out mostly 4G phones for this comparison, however, we just don’t review that many 4G phones – so, we had no choice but to put the Redmi Note 17 4G against a group of mostly 5G phones.
As promised, we start with the comparison between the 4G and 5G versions of the Redmi Note 17. They have largely the same hardware except for the chipset – the 4G model gets a 6nm Snapdragon 6s 4G Gen 2, while the 5G model gets a 4nm Snapdragon 4 Gen 4. Both phones have 7,700mAh Si/C batteries with 45W charging and huge 6.99” displays (1080p+, 120Hz).
Video playback is also quite a bit shorter and the web browsing time is an hour down. So, even if you don’t care about 5G connectivity, you should have a good look at the price difference between the two models. The 5G version offers longer battery life and slightly better performance.
This time around, Xiaomi skipped the 4G Pro model, but there was one last generation – the Redmi Note 15 Pro 4G. The combo of a smaller screen (6.77”) and a smaller battery (6,500mAh) works out to an Active Use Score that comes close to the Note 17 4G. However, the Helio G200 Ultra (6nm) chipset didn’t do great in the gaming and web browsing tests.
The Redmi Note 17 Pro is only available in 5G form and while it has a larger battery of 8,340mAh than the vanilla Note 17 models (+640mAh), its Active Use Score is lower than both phones.
The Redmi Note 15 4G and 5G had 6,000mAh and 5,520mAh batteries, respectively, paired with 33W and 45W charging. In other words, the old 4G model got a larger battery that had slower charging than its 5G sibling. This year’s Note 17 4G and 5G are on par in both respects.
After a quick 15-minute charge, both phones were at 23%. At the 30-minute mark, the two phones were still on the same level (save for some rounding error). What’s interesting is that the old Redmi Note 15 Pro 4G didn’t pull ahead, even though it has a smaller battery (6,500mAh vs. 7,700mAh) and the same 45W charge rating.
Meanwhile, the new Pro (5G) has a bigger battery (+8%) and faster charging (+49%). These two balance out and the Pro model is on nearly the same charge level as the two vanilla Redmi Note 17 phones. You may think that 8% and 49% are not balanced, but the reality is that the peak charging rating isn’t what determines charge time – the charge curve does that.
This brings us to the third tab – the Redmi Note 17 4G needed an hour and a half for a full charge. The 5G model got there three minutes quicker, but that is within the margin of error – we don’t think that the different chipsets had any impact here.
The Redmi Note 17 Pro (8,340mAh, 67W) beat the Realme 16T (8,000mAh, 45W) to the finish line, despite having a bigger battery. We think the vanilla Note 17 models would have benefited from 67W charging support too as an hour and a half feels sluggish.
The Redmi Note 17 4G uses a Snapdragon 6s 4G Gen 2 chipset – this is actually a downgrade from the Note 15 4G, which had a Helio G100 Ultra, which benchmarked better than the Snapdragon.
So, this is a downgrade or, if we’re being generous, a side-grade. The old model also had a 108MP main camera (1/1.67”) that was replaced by a basic 50MP module (1/2.76”). And sure, the new model has a larger 6.99” display (vs. 6.77”), but whether you consider having a huge phone (that is 79.1mm wide!) an upgrade or not is up to you.
Here’s what we think – the larger battery capacity on the Redmi Note 17 4G gives it better battery life, which is a solid upgrade. But the 5G version lasts even longer and both charge at the same speed (which we still think could have been improved).
The global pricing of the Note 17 4G is $250, while the 5G variant is $50 more. In Europe, the difference in MSRPs is €100, but after discounts you are looking at €230 for a 4/128GB 4G and €300 for a 6/128GB 5G model (an 8% off coupon got that down to €276). In India, the 4G model didn’t even launch, there’s only the 5G model.
The price gap between the 4G and the 5G phones is not small, but we think it’s worth it for the better performance and longer battery life of the Redmi Note 17 5G. And if you don’t like that one, you can always just get the Poco M8 Pro. The 4G model feels out of place and out of time.
You're right, the modem is fine. I've fixed the article.

23,2026-10-04,"Nintendo's rich console history is quite deep and interesting, even if not all of its gaming systems made it outside of Japan. In some cases, Nintendo didn't find the market strong enough to warrant a global release, and in others, some of the company's inventions were simply too niche. Looking back, Nintendo's diverse portfolio is intriguing, given how large its global gaming presence became, compared to when the company first established its console lineup. Today, it would be highly unusual to see any Nintendo console or similar invention be permanently stuck in Japan.
For example, Nintendo and Panasonic released a very rare Nintendo GameCube edition that was Japan-exclusive, dubbed the Panasonic Q GameCube. This item, if sold today, can fetch well over $1,000, depending on the condition and what's included. It turns out that this limited-run edition had very few units sold in Japan – less than 100,000 – and is considered a rare collector's item. But this also meant that there was basically no chance that this niche product would ever leave the country.
The Twin Famicom, also known as the Sharp Twin Famicom, is a 2-in-1 console designed by Sharp in 1986. The Japan-exclusive system combines Nintendo's Family Computer, also known as the Famicom, cartridge slot and the Famicom Disk System (FDS) into one package. These units were released in two slightly different color schemes: red with black trim or black with red trim. The system also included built-in RCA connectors, while still including an external RF modulator as an add-on. Previous Famicom systems were also RF-only, so this one technically offered an audio and video upgrade if the TV could support it. The back of the console also includes two hard-wired controllers with a D-pad and face buttons — though turbo switches were later added to the AN-505 models and the controllers had longer cables.
If you want to procure a Twin Famicom, you can do so on eBay. Conditions will vary, as most sellers have them used and without the original retail box. You may also find the console in a retro store in Japan, or you can import one through Enjoy Game Japan online.
The Panasonic Q GameCube was definitely a special-if-not-quirky gem released in the early 2000s, based on a Nintendo collaboration with Panasonic. It was also Nintendo's answer to a console that could natively play DVDs/CDs, much like its Sony rival. However, this premium console see as much success as one could hope. One reason is because its launch price was nearly ¥41,000 to ¥46,000, and Japanese consumers found it cheaper to buy a GameCube and a DVD player separately. Nintendo didn't fully advertise the product when the regular GameCube launched, and Panasonic also didn't give it the proper marketing it needed to get off the ground — plus, it didn't help that the regular Nintendo GameCube was also a commercial failure. Then, of course, while the chassis has a nice, shiny, unique coating and structure — looking almost like a metallic mini stereo and oven — it wasn't compatible with many accessories. All of that combined, essentially made this rare collector's item a commercial failure, which is why it likely never left Japan.
Finding a Panasonic Q GameCube at a reasonable price is trickier because of its rarity. You can find these units sold internationally on eBay, but at a hefty premium for a working model, and cheaper if you buy one for parts.
When mentioning the Famicom, or any iteration of it, things tend to get a little confusing, because the Nintendo Entertainment System (NES) is technically just the westernized version of the Famicom. That's why it makes more sense to look at specific Famicom editions that never made it to the west, like the Nintendo AV Famicom. The Nintendo AV Famicom was released in 1993. It was considered a rebuild of the 1983 Famicom. The major difference came from the technical specs of the console: Nintendo used this revision to replace the old RF-only TV hookup with standard composite AV multi-out ports. That's also why the Nintendo AV Famicom differs from the newer NES (NES-101) that came overseas in 1993; Nintendo decided to cut the AV ports and reverted to a strict RF modulator output. This was likely down to a financial decision to cut costs and maximize profit at the end of the Famicom/NES era, since the SNES was already out in Japan and America in 1990 and 1991, respectively.
The Nintendo AV Famicom isn't too difficult to find since it isn't as old as some of the other Japanese-exclusive consoles on this list. The easiest place to look is eBay, where you can search for the model (HV-101), or you can use Enjoy Game Japan to import one.
Nintendo's origin is Japan. So it's no surprise that Nintendo's original console, the one that predates the original Famicom, also never left Japan. The Color TV-Game has a whole series that started in the late 1970s and continued until 1980, and it was developed alongside Mitsubishi Electronics. More notably, it wasn't like a traditional gaming console where you bought cartridges and loaded them to play; these came with preloaded games.
The Color TV-Game 6, released in 1977, included six variations of ""Pong,"" including hockey, volleyball, and light tennis. Later, the Color TV-Game 15 came out, with 15 variations and detachable controllers. The Color TV-Game Racing 112, followed in 1978, featuring a top-down racing game. In the following years, 1979 and 1980, the Color TV-Game Block Breaker and Computer TV-Game (based on the arcade game ""Computer Othello"") focused more on specific arcade titles. It also makes sense that Nintendo never exported these inventions outside Japan because, at the time, the company was still focused on the domestic market and lacked the resources to go international. Nintendo of America was only established in 1980.
The Color TV-Game series is a little trickier to secure today. It is quite old, but it also lives up to its name. You can buy them separately on eBay, but you will have a hard time finding the Color TV-Game Block Breaker and Computer TV-Game. Fortunately, museums also preserve the latter two systems, such as the Centre for Computing History for Color TV-Game Block Breaker and the Mario Museum for Computer TV-Game."

Asus's new Wi-Fi 8 beast gives your Wi-Fi 7 device an advantage

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Asus has beaten every other manufacturer to market with the ROG Rapture GT-BN98, the world's first commercially available Wi-Fi 8 gaming router.
It's a bold flagship release for a wireless standard that isn't even finalized yet — and with zero Wi-Fi 8 client devices currently on sale, reviewers had to get creative just to test what it can actually do. I dug into three in-depth reviews to see whether it's worth buying now.
Build quality drew consistent praise. It's ""extremely well constructed with very good attention to detail,"" said Blacktubi, while Guru3D leaned into the router's dramatic looks, joking that unboxing it feels like ""a small alien spacecraft has crash-landed on the desk.""
YouTuber Landpet was blunt about the performance side, calling it flat-out ""an absolute beast of a router.""
On paper, the GT-BN98's theoretical link rates are identical to its Wi-Fi 7 predecessor, the GT-BE98 — Blacktubi pointed out the headline numbers ""are the exact same."" But real-world range and throughput told a different story.
Hiding it would be counterproductive, since the antennas require space to be positioned correctly and the chassis benefits from unobstructed airflow
The chassis is large by any standard. It measures roughly 35cm by 35cm and weighs over 2kg. It needs ""a proper, dedicated spot"" rather than a small shelf, and ruling out wall mounting altogether given the size.
The antennas dominate the look, adjustable around the perimeter and giving the router an almost insect-like silhouette when fully extended. The top panel mixes a metallic finish with geometric detailing and translucent panels, plus the expected Aura RGB lighting.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
Build quality feels solid throughout — no flex, clean panel fit, antenna arms that hold their position. Front-edge LEDs give a quick status read without opening the app.
Thermally, this Asus router added a thicker aluminum top plate and an upgraded heatsink layer for passive cooling. Stress testing turned up no signs of throttling.
Inside is a 2.6GHz quad-core processor and 2GB of RAM, running a quad-band radio setup — 2.4GHz, dual 5GHz bands, and a single 6GHz band — for a combined theoretical throughput of 25,000Mbps.
A North America-only ""Pro"" variant swaps the second 5GHz band for an extra 6GHz band on the UNII-8 spectrum, which isn't approved for use in most other regions.
For wired gear, there are two 10GbE ports (one general-purpose, one dedicated gaming port), four 2.5GbE ports, and a single gigabit port, with up to six LAN ports active depending on configuration. USB 3.2 Gen1 and USB 2.0 round things out for network storage or 4G/5G backup internet.
This is officially the fastest wireless backhaul router that I've tested, which is insane
Real-world speed testing — done entirely with Wi-Fi 7 client devices, since Wi-Fi 8 hardware doesn't exist yet — found wired and wireless backhaul holding up strongly even at 100 feet away
Over a wired 10G connection, speeds matched his 5 Gbps ISP plan exactly in both directions. On Wi-Fi, internet speeds hit 4.1 Gbps down and 2 Gbps up.
Local network testing — where speed isn't capped by the ISP — pushed even higher: 4.6 Gbps down and 4.2 Gbps up over wireless backhaul between two mesh units, with wired backhaul close behind at 4.5 Gbps down and 4.2 Gbps up.
Range held up well too. At 20ft, speeds stayed around 4 Gbps down; at 50ft outdoors, 2.7 Gbps down; and even at 100ft, it still managed 1.92 Gbps down, though upload dropped sharply to 718 Mbps.
A more controlled comparison, pairing two GT-BN98 units against two GT-BE98 units, found ""significantly higher"" throughput at long range with the newer hardware.
The more surprising finding: those gains aren't limited to future Wi-Fi 8 gear. Testing with an existing Wi-Fi 7 MacBook still measured ""more than 10% higher average throughput"" on the GT-BN98 versus the older Wi-Fi router — evidence that upgraded radio components benefit today's devices too, even without any Wi-Fi 8 features actually switched on.
The ASUSWRT 6.0 was singled out as a real step up, with the interface ""noticeably more modern and much more responsive"" than Asus's decade-old firmware.
The headline addition is the new Adaptive QoE engine, which ""smartly allocates network resources to balance gaming, streaming, and daily tasks,"" replacing the older Adaptive QoS system.
Security also got an upgrade via the revamped AiProtection suite, which was called ""genuinely impressive"" for including network-level ad and tracker blocking at no extra cost.
The new Wi-Fi Insight tool also correctly flagged interference from a Zigbee smart home devices on the 2.4GHz band, a decent addition which impressively “works as intended”.
Pricing is still rolling out region by region ahead of the router's wider October 2026 launch.
The flagship GT-BN98 Pro is currently the only model on sale, listed on ASUS's Canadian eStore at CA$1,299.99.
A US launch price of around $899 has been floated by early previews but isn't yet official.
The standard GT-BN98 (non-Pro) has also been priced at €960 in Europe, though North American pricing for that specific model hasn't been confirmed yet.
Given the router is shipping ahead of Wi-Fi 8's official IEEE finalization — not expected until 2028 — buyers are paying an early-adopter premium on top of already-flagship router pricing.
This is a router for enthusiasts buying ahead of the curve, not something you need for Wi-Fi 8 today — compatible client devices aren't expected until 2027 at the earliest.
Still, the benefits aren't purely theoretical: better range, backhaul speed, and firmware improvements apply right now, even to existing Wi-Fi 7 devices.
The good
- Meaningfully better range and throughput, even on older Wi-Fi 7 devices
- Premium build quality with no thermal throttling under load
- Adaptive QoE and free-included ad/tracker blocking are genuine upgrades
The bad
- No Wi-Fi 8 client devices exist yet to fully prove the standard
- Large footprint requires dedicated desk space
- High price tag
The verdict
Worth it if you want the best router hardware available today and don't mind future-proofing ahead of the Wi-Fi 8 ecosystem. Hold off if you're not running multi-gig internet or don't need flagship-level range right now.

26,2026-10-04,"Nvidia has raised the price of the Shield TV Pro by $100 to $299.99, effective immediately, according to a company spokesperson. The Android TV streaming box launched in 2019 at $199.99, and Nvidia attributes the increase to higher component prices, memory in particular.
""Starting October 2, SHIELD Pro will be priced at $299. The cost of components, including memory, has increased substantially across the industry,"" the Nvidia spokesperson said.
The non-Pro Shield TV, which launched at $149.99, has already been discontinued, so the Shield TV Pro is the only model left in the lineup.
The current Shield TV Pro still runs the Tegra X1+ processor it shipped with in 2019. It supports AI upscaling and hardware decoding for almost any type of media, which has kept it relevant for home theater setups years after release.
Nvidia's Andrew Bell said earlier this year that the company still actively manufactures the Shield and sells every unit it makes. That means Nvidia has no stockpile of older hardware to sell at the previous price.
New units go through the same supply chain as current smartphones and PCs, where anything with memory or storage has become more expensive since generative AI reshaped technology supply chains. Nvidia itself is a central supplier of AI accelerators and GPUs, which places the company on both sides of the component squeeze.
Stock of the Shield TV Pro has been limited for months. The $299.99 price gives Nvidia room to produce more units at current component costs. The device is still listed as out of stock on Nvidia's own store, while Best Buy sells it at the new price.
Gaming hardware has gone through the same pattern, with existing products getting more expensive during their lifetimes. Nintendo, Microsoft, and Sony have all raised prices on current consoles, and the PS5 Pro has moved from $699.99 at its 2024 launch to $899.99.
At $299.99, the Shield TV Pro is now harder to justify for basic streaming. Buyers can use these checks before ordering:
Nvidia has not said whether the price will drop if memory costs ease, and the company has not announced a successor to the 2019 hardware.

28,2026-10-04,"PIMCO cofounder Bill Gross, who revolutionized bond investing with active trading strategies, warned the overall credit landscape has become unbalanced and cautioned against holding longer-term debt.
In a Financial Times op-ed on Wednesday, he pointed out that government, mortgage and corporate credit now totals about $84 trillion. 
“Too much debt can lead to too much risk and too much equity can lead to less earnings per share growth under certain underperforming productivity cycles,” Gross wrote. “Move them both at the same pace consistent with industry standards and economic growth more than likely expands as well.”
But balance sheets have grown too lopsided, putting growth at risk, he said. The AI sector’s debt boom is an anomaly by historical standards, and federal debt has already hit peak levels for peacetime, now at 100% of GDP.
While all that debt is fueling growth now, it has led to higher inflation today and will likely slow growth in the future, Gross added.
“In such an environment, my view is: don’t own bonds, with the exception of one-year Treasury bills, which are now at 4.55%,” he said. “Be cautious with stocks at record levels as higher yields over time will contract profit margins. Be prepared for the end of ‘what you are used to’ stock markets and higher volatility in prices for the benchmark 10-year Treasury bonds.”
His admonition is notable given his career in bond investing, which earned him the moniker “Bond King.” For decades, he dominated a corner of financial markets that was considered sleepy before he arrived on the scene.
Rather than simply buying bonds and holding them to maturity to collect interest, his investment strategies generated returns well beyond what “clipping coupons” provided.
But in recent years, the market has experienced its own transformation as well. Central banks around the world no longer reliably buy and hold Treasury debt as they seek to diversify their reserves. At the same time, price-sensitive hedge funds have emerged as bigger players in the bond market and are quicker to sell. 
The so-called basis trade that’s become popular among hedge funds, where they profit from small price differences between Treasury bonds and the Treasury futures, has made the market more volatile.
In fact, the basis trade has grown so much that hedge funds’ share of total Treasury holdings has almost doubled since 2023 to 8.5%, exceeding the portion that depository institutions and mutual funds command.
The new era of volatility has been on display this year, as 10-year Treasury yields have soared more than 100 basis points since the Iran war started and recently hit the highest levels in 24 years. 
While hedge funds are a key source of liquidity in the market, they may weaken bonds’ reputation as a safe-haven asset, Joe Maher, markets economist at Capital Economics, said in a note in August.
“In a risk-off environment, safe-haven flows into sovereign bonds may be offset by hedge funds unwinding their leveraged trading positions as funding conditions tighten,” he wrote. “And given they have no obligation to act as market makers, the more likely it is that liquidity dries up in these markets in times of stress.”
On top of that, hedge funds could transmit stress across different assets, Maher warned. For example, a stock market selloff could force hedge funds to dump bond positions to cover their losses in equities.
For his part, Gross said he is suspicious of AI hyperscalers, unless they have price-to-earnings ratios of less than 20. And while stocks like Verizon and AT&T have decent yields, their mobile phone businesses are threatened by SpaceX’s Starlink.
Some income funds that are trading at a discount to net asset values may offer some opportunities, but they would suffer if short-term interest rates rise higher than expected, he added.
“Preserve and protect is my current investment motto,” Gross wrote.
Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing.

31,2026-10-04,"Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily.
As I have dozens of times before, I recently braved our local Sephora to pick up a gift for one of the many kids’ birthday parties on our shared family calendar. But this time, when I handed the bottle of trendy hair oil to my 14-year-old daughter, I got a new response. “I can’t give this to Maggie,” my daughter protested.
“It’s nice!” I said, a tad defensively. “The salesperson recommended it.”
“I mean, I like it. But it’s not on her Giftful.”
I forced a long, deep breath, suppressing the lecture forming in my mind. Giftful and its counterparts Giftster, GiftList, and WishApp are gift-registry apps, and at least one has a prominent place on the home screen of every teenage girl I know. Year-round, the girls add product links that specify their preferred color, size, brand, and price—then share their lists with friends and family for birthdays and holiday parties.

As apps go, Giftful seems pretty harmless. And it has its uses. Parents of young kids can share lists with grandparents, who otherwise might spend excessively on unwanted or duplicate toys. And when a pre-selected gift is a literal click away, parents who think ahead can be saved a trip to the ninth circle of hell (I am referring, obviously, to the local mall) on a hectic Saturday morning before a birthday party. In theory, an app like Giftful can prevent waste and increase efficiency—both good things.
But we’re relying on efficiency as a measure of value an awful lot these days. As someone who has always cherished meaningful gift exchanges, I’d hoped the realm of gift giving might be spared the pressure of optimization. I’d hoped we might, for reasons of sentimentality or connection, preserve some human practices in their inefficient forms. If the wish list’s popularity among teenagers is any indication, Gen Z and Gen Alpha have other ideas. I can’t help but think ubiquitous gift registries may be distorting the concept of gift giving for entire generations of kids.
The issue goes deeper than presents. I have four kids from elementary to high-school age, and as hard as I try to expose them to life’s realities, they remain sheltered from disappointment and dissatisfaction at every turn. Netflix curates their media, I accommodate their food preferences, teachers personalize their education, and now, increasingly, when someone gives them a present, it’s something my kid has declared she wants. I understand the temptation to text an Amazon link to Grandma. But Giftful and its ilk take this a step further, with the kids themselves curating “wish lists” from an infinite stream of products offered up by their smartphones. The experience of the app creates a powerful illusion of consumer sovereignty, which is not something I necessarily need my teenage daughter to have.
Gen Z and Gen Alpha are famously savvy consumers, and acquiring things has never been easier. My own closet is proof that shopping can feel perilously frictionless. Gifts, though, have remained a unique form of buying culture because, historically, the recipient hasn’t been able to control the entire transaction. More than a commercial exchange, a gift can still be surprising, strange, or revelatory. It can also be impractical or disappointing, but that low-stakes “risk” is what makes it fun. Plus, learning how to graciously respond to unwanted gifts is a cornerstone of every kid’s social education. A moment of disappointment is a brief but meaningful chance for a kid to decenter herself before tearing the wrapping off yet another box on Christmas morning.
But registries like Giftful leach the magic out of the gifting process and forestall the need for humility and even gratitude. The giver morphs from someone who interprets the recipient into someone who fulfills the recipient’s specifications. Disappointment becomes another problem to engineer away. In a world that is already hyperpersonalized to kids’ tastes, I worry these apps are yet another signal that intimacy is something that happens online, by way of corporate brands, and doesn’t come in the strange wrapping paper Grandma bought at the dollar store. One of the most meaningful gifts I received as a child was a volume of Emily Dickinson’s poems, but I didn’t love the book at first. It took me years to realize my mother had chosen the gift for a version of me—the writer—she understood I’d one day become.
Registries make the most sense when a person needs something in particular; they’ve been used for brides and grooms and soon-to-be parents for decades. To set up a young adult for a new life, it helps to know whether they have or do not have a whisk, a set of forks, a shower curtain. The logic of need does not apply to teenage girls in the market for a seasonal Jellycat plushie or peptide lip tint.
Some of my daughter’s friendships seem to lack the intimacy and silliness that marked my teenage relationships. The teenagers I observe today seem less willing to make themselves vulnerable or take an emotional risk, and groups present as more homogeneous: one hairstyle, one outfit, one shoe brand. Much of this can be traced to online habits, which prepare teenagers to behave and dress and shop according to a set of trends. Methods of signaling adolescent clanship have never been so fixed—or so expensive—as in the era of the wish list.
Inside Giftful, the Explore tab opens into a world of recommendations, so teens can realize they need all manner of things they hadn’t heard of before. Users learn to identify inside the structure of certain brands and classify themselves inside ready-made categories. Wish list curations have titles like “Vanilla Girl,” “Cottagecore,” “Y2K Girl,” “Street Drip,” “E-girl,” and “Old Money Aesthetic.” Does my daughter really like ruffle socks and cable-knit cardigans, or did she fall too far down the “Classic Preppy” rabbit hole? I don’t know, but—and this is far more telling—I doubt she does either.
Of course, to a certain extent, a self-centered, dog-eat-dog, conformist mentality is normal among teenagers. But as a parent, I want to actively encourage my kids in the opposite direction, toward friendships built around shared interests, not shared brands. I want their relationships to form based on what makes them different, not how good they are at being the same.
When I was amply pregnant with twins and didn’t think I could survive another day, my older sister gave me 50 tiny numbered gifts to get through the final 50 days, along with explicit instructions to open one a day. The gifts ranged from a package of Junior Mints to a handwritten joke to a printout of Tim Riggins (Friday Night Lights was at its peak) with a text bubble that said, “Hang in there, Linds.”
Each gift took me about 10 seconds to open every day, but she must have spent hours assembling 50 of them. Each little slip of paper or trinket was a tangible reminder of my sister’s care, which was just what I needed to survive one of the hardest seasons of my life. That kind of thoughtful gift giving isn’t innate; it’s practiced. Anti-commercial gift giving is a set of skills I want my kids to learn: thinking ahead, paying close attention to another person, anticipating needs, sacrificing resources other than cash. To give a thoughtful gift, you have to do more than click a wish list; you have to get inside another person’s skin.
This isn’t an indictment of parents who use registries to keep grandparents from buying yet another stuffed animal or even the teenagers assembling commodity-filled fun. Techies are geniuses at creating apps that respond to our “pain points,” and awkward or superfluous gift giving can be a source of discomfort. But when we overrely on efficiency, we push out newness and surprise. We reduce the chances that our kid taps into an unknown love of harmonica playing because Uncle Jeff took a gift-giving gamble. A long shot, sure. But long shots are the spice of life.
If we don’t draw boundaries around the market economy, the ethos of it can seep into our every interaction, defining not just our shopping habits but the quality of our relationships, too. It seems particularly important to me that kids recognize the seepage when it happens, that if they are going to browse Giftful, they at least know they are being tracked, their habits tabulated and recycled back to them as preferences. Kids who can’t sense they are being manipulated and targeted are the most susceptible to the myth that joy is found primarily in the marketplace.
My younger daughter, who is 12, is still phoneless (and, therefore, app-less). She’s spent the last two weeks assembling a basket of items for her best friend’s upcoming birthday. It includes a goofy photo of the girls we printed at Walgreens for 39 cents, a fish keychain that I’m told is a reference to an inside joke, a homemade card on printer paper, and a bag of Lay’s pickle chips.
I don’t understand the gift, nor would I want to receive it. And that’s just the point. It isn’t for me. It is, very specifically, for my daughter’s best friend Sophie. The gift is not really the pickle chips, which cost $2.50. The gift is being known.

32,2026-10-04,"
Somewhere in a closet, a perfectly good LEGO minifigure is staring through a plastic bag at the back of a cardboard box. It has a tiny printed face, a surprisingly elaborate hat, and absolutely no audience. Every serious LEGO collector knows the feeling: shelves fill up fast, display cases sell at fixed sizes, and at some point the collection starts living in labeled ziplock bags rather than anywhere it can actually be seen. LEGO has released over 775 Collectible Minifigures across 51 series since 2010, with new figures arriving three times a year, which makes the storage problem a permanent one for anyone collecting seriously. Stephen Peaple, a British-born collector now based in Perth, Australia, had thousands of them and spent four years building the solution he could not find anywhere.
Finity Frames puts a collection on the wall in a clean white grid. A single frame carries 16 spaces, but removable dividers let you make room for bigger figures or pack in a crowd. When the collection grows, connected frames share edges so the display extends as one continuous piece without visual seams or doubled-up borders. Your next minifigure gets a spot without requiring a complete wall makeover. The system is injection-molded ABS, ships flat-packed, and the connection method that makes all of it work is patented.
Designer: Stephen Peaple
Click Here to Buy Now: $55. Hurry, only 5/16 left!

Every Finity Frame is built around a seamless edge connection where neighboring frames share a single border, eliminating the doubled-up edge and any visual gap at the join. This is the core innovation that solves the fixed-size problem inherent in every other display case on the market. It means you can start with a single frame for one series of figures and expand outward in any direction, at any time, without having to replace your original investment. The aesthetic is clean and continuous, looking like a single, cohesive unit no matter how large it gets.
This design approach also makes the system more efficient as it grows. Peaple makes the math explicit: the first frame measures 312mm across, and each connected frame adds just 298mm, because one edge is always shared. This reduces material waste and lowers the cost per additional frame. A 2×2 Collector, for instance, cannot be broken apart into four 1×1 Starters, since it doesn’t include the extra pieces. For context, the most prominent fixed-case competitors max out around 190 minifigures. The Finity Frames Showcase holds 1,056, and two of them together can display over 2,100 figures as a single, unified piece.


Each Finity Frame ships with three horizontal and three vertical internal slats, forming a standard 4×4 grid. This sixteen-space layout is not arbitrary; it maps exactly to one complete Collectible Minifigures series, which traditionally ships with 16 unique figures. The slats are removable in any combination, yielding 64 different internal layouts. You can pull the vertical dividers out, double-stack figures with jumper plates, and a 1×1 Starter can hold up to 88 minifigures. This flexibility lets you create custom spaces for dioramas, larger vehicles, or oversized figures like the Hulk who need a little extra room.
The system’s dimensions are precisely calibrated to the LEGO System of Play. Internal slats are exactly one brick wide, and the frame edges are exactly two bricks wide. Each compartment accepts a 6×8 LEGO plate or four 3×4 minifigure plates, the same stands that come with CMF packs. This means removing the acrylic cover turns the frame into a wall-mounted baseplate where builds can bridge continuously across spaces and frames. Peaple calls this “bridging,” a feature that turns a static display into a creative canvas. Every horizontal slat even has a built-in row of six studs for securing figures or small builds.


A magnetic Perspex acrylic cover protects the collection from dust and removes in a single pull. It’s made from UV-stable material with built-in absorbers to block harmful light, preventing the slow fading that can damage valuable figures over time. The frame itself is a gallery-white, UV-treated ABS plastic, a color choice meant to disappear behind the collection rather than compete with it. Everything is designed to last, preserving the collection while keeping it easily accessible. The magnetic attachment is secure but quick, so you can get to your figures without fussing with clips or latches.
Flipping the horizontal slats 180 degrees turns the studded surfaces into flat shelves, opening the frame to almost any small-scale collectible. A single 64x64x49mm space can hold Funko Pocket POPs, Be@rbricks, Sonny Angels, or even shot glasses. Removing dividers creates double-height spaces for larger items like vintage Kenner 3¾” figures, Super7 ReAction figures, Playmobil sets, or graded collectible cards in their hard cases. It’s a true cabinet of curiosities, designed for a LEGO collection but adaptable enough for just about anything else that deserves a spot on the wall.


The five frame sizes run from the 1×1 Starter at 12.3×12.3 inches (up to 88 minifigures) to the 4×3 Showcase at 47.4×35.7 inches (up to 1,056), with the 1×2 Duo, 2×2 Collector, and 3×2 Lifestyle filling in between. All frames run 55mm deep without the cover, 61mm with, and production units are estimated at 0.9 to 1.1kg for the 1×1 (roughly 2 lbs).
Retail pricing starts at AU$99 (roughly US$65) for the Starter and reaches AU$835 (roughly US$550) for the Showcase. Any size can be extended later through a Frame Extender on finityframes.com, where you input what you already own and order only the additional sets required. The system ships flat-packed globally, with US distribution in place through the manufacturing partner.

Windows 11 26H2 goes stable, Surface’s October 7 event looms, and Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X2 outperforms Intel — it’s been a week of quiet but meaningful shifts across Microsoft’s ecosystem.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

 



It looks like Microsoft is wrapping up its Windows and Surface efforts for 2026 to be able to focus on the future. Last week the company announced refreshes to the Surface Pro 12-inch and Surface Laptop 13-inch. This week, Microsoft rolled out Windows 11 version 26H2.
With a bunch of Surface announcements in the rearview mirror and another update to Windows 11 rolling out, Microsoft can point its followers to the future. The description of the Windows and Surface event next week says, ""Join us live... for a look at what's next from Windows, Surface, and NVIDIA.""
I hope Microsoft has some exciting news in store for its event. Windows and Surface have been far from exciting lately. It is possible to market boring products, of course, but they have to be reliable.
This week Microsoft announced Windows 11 version 26H2 and called it a ""predictable and low-disruption update."" If Microsoft wants to win back consumers, it needs to either be cutting edge or actually deliver predictable and low-disruption updates. It feels like for the last few years we've gotten neither.
The next version of Windows 11 is now generally available. Here's what's now and how you can get it.
Microsoft's October 7 Windows and Surface event is almost here. Here's where to watch and what to expect.
New tests show Qualcomm’s 18‑core Snapdragon X2 outperforming Intel’s mainstream laptop chips across CPU, AI, battery life, and price, proving the non‑Extreme model is already a major threat.
Image credit: Qualcomm
Windows 11 version 26H2 is the last officially supported version of Windows for the Snapdragon 850 SoC.
Setting up Linux environments on Windows 11 often requires third-party tools, but native WSL container streamlines the process.
With Windows 11 version 26H1 and 26H2 built on different Windows codebases, Microsoft is planning to bring everyone back onto the same platform release with version 27H2 in 2027.
Microsoft has had to release multiple out-of-band updates in 2026. Some bugs required multiple patches to fix. It's gotten so bad that most of our articles about Windows 11 updates are met with comments asking what will break this time.
Unfortunately, the commenters have been proven right several times. One serious bug made Outlook, OneDrive, Dropbox, and other cloud-backed apps inoperable. Another issue froze Remote Desktop and caused audio failures. At one point users couldn't even install a Windows 11 update, forcing Microsoft to pull that update.
If Microsoft is going to undercut its events and trim down its Surface lineup, it needs to double down on reliability. Next week, we should get some answers about Microsoft's plans.
 



A boring but bug-free Windows 11 update may be a welcome change of pace, but it makes me curious about what Microsoft will discuss at its Windows and Surface event next week. Considering Windows 11 version 26H2 is already rolling out, the focus is likely the future of Windows.
Does Microsoft have something up its sleeve? Has the tech giant learned a lesson about how to build consumer hype? Or are we due for another on-stage announcement about AI and how new PCs can run models locally?
I'm sure there are developers interested in the potential of the Surface Laptop Ultra and RTX Spark for AI development, but there are hundreds of millions of Windows users who care very little about AI. Microsoft needs to at least throw some scraps our way to keep us interested.
Amazon's October Prime Day is next week, but there are already some great deals live. Competing retailers are trying to beat Amazon to the punch, resulting in big savings.
A trio of laptops are the best deals of the week. I'm partial toward the Lenovo Yoga Slim 7x because of its thin design but the Samsung Galaxy Book6 and Dell 14S are also great choices.

34,2026-10-04,"Sharp rise in rents triggers mass protests, but conservative MPs vote against measures to tackle the crisis.
Save
Share
Tens of thousands of people have rallied across Spain as protests have spread since the conservative-dominated parliament rejected a bill proposing measures to tackle a housing crisis.
Parliament on Friday voted down two decrees proposed by Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez’s minority left-wing coalition government that were intended to address the housing crisis.
The protests erupted late last month after an 87-year-old woman was evicted from her home of 70 years in the capital, Madrid. Maria del Carmen Abascal’s eviction came after an international investment fund bought her building and hiked the rent.
Local media reports have suggested that Sanchez could call snap elections for as early as November after watching the strength of Saturday’s protests across the country. Spain’s current legislative term is set to end next summer.
Will the housing crisis trigger snap elections? Here’s what we know:
The emergency measures proposed by Spain’s leftist government to tackle the country’s housing affordability crisis failed to pass on Friday.
The government had proposed two measures to protect renters in the country. The first decree was meant to stop evictions of vulnerable people, make real estate speculation harder, impose tighter restrictions on short-term leases and provide tax incentives to encourage renting and the construction of housing.
The second decree proposed making leases automatically renew when they are set to expire, essentially making them indefinite. A property owner would have to pay a tenant 12 months of rent to break the contract.
Sanchez, who leads a coalition government composed of his Socialist Party and the far-left Sumar, ⁠appealed to lawmakers in the run-up to the vote to put aside political calculations and pressure.
“Think about ordinary people, about those who most need the protection of public institutions. Think about the millions of young people who ⁠cannot afford to ⁠move out of their parents’ homes. Think about the families living with the constant ⁠anxiety of ever-rising rents,” he said before the voting.
But his government’s proposed measures were shot down by the majority of ⁠the 350-seat lower house of parliament.
The right-wing ⁠Catalan separatist ⁠party Junts refused to back the housing decrees, saying they would ultimately tighten the housing market. The centre-right Popular Party and far-right Vox also voted against the measures.
Miriam Nogueras of Junts argued the decrees would lead only to owners removing their properties from the rental market and thus driving up prices even further.
“If they really wanted to protect people, collar the vulture funds, and reactivate housing, they would have already done it. They’ve had eight years,” she added on X, referring to Sanchez’s government.
Health Minister Monica Garcia said on X that the right-wing parties “have voted for a feudal economy in which a minority extracts the salary from a majority that works for their luxuries. And they do it against their own voters, who also suffer increasingly high rents, live in fear that the contract will end and can even be evicted.”
“Today the right has given rent-seeking a parliamentary majority that it doesn’t have on the street, but it’s going to pay for it sooner rather than later,” she added.
Before the parliamentary vote, Marta Soler Gallart, head of the Department of Sociology at the University of Barcelona, explained that neither of the two decrees addressed the housing problem in the medium or long term.
“Models such as Vienna’s with its low rents are cited in support of these decrees, but it is overlooked that the Vienna model and others have low rents because of their long history of creating and maintaining public social housing,” Soler Gallart told Al Jazeera on September 30.
“In Spain, however, public funds are invested in social housing which, after a while, the recipients sell at market price.”
Soler Gallart also said the new measures disadvantage landlords with small or few properties by extending five-year tenancy agreements by a further two years and by providing an indefinite extension of their leases.
“However, these extensions are not compulsory if the landlord can prove that they need the property for their own use,” she said.
Protests erupted across Spain in late September after 87-year-old Abascal was evicted from her home. The investment fund that bought her building hiked her rent by more than 200 percent to roughly $1,880 a month, a move aimed at pushing out the long-term tenant to make way for lucrative tourist apartments.
In recent years, Spain has seen protests against mass tourism, which has been linked to the housing crisis and soaring cost of living. Spain is the second most visited country in the world, attracting 97 million tourists last year.
While Abascal has returned to her home, according to her representatives, she has become the face of the country’s housing crisis, and people across Spain have been demanding the government address the issue.
Angered by parliament’s rejection of the government measures, tens of thousands of people took to the streets.
“It’s a shame,” Nando de Anguulo, 26, who joined Saturday’s protest in Madrid, told the Reuters news agency.
“It only proves that it is us, the people, who have to adapt to the political parties and not the politicians to what the people want, and this can’t go on like that.”
While the protests were mostly peaceful, in the southeastern city of Valencia, police fired rubber bullets and tear gas to break up demonstrations. About 30 protesters managed to get inside the City of Arts and Sciences, a landmark cultural building, before police removed them.
Meanwhile, in Madrid, Spanish police arrested three members of a far-right group and fired rubber bullets on Saturday night.
Far-right activists, some of whom performed Nazi salutes, tried to reach demonstrators camped out in the Puerta del Sol, a central square in the Spanish capital, the Spanish government said.
Valeria Racu, a spokesperson for the Tenants’ Union, said Saturday’s rallies revealed “a new energy that we haven’t seen in a long time and energy that is showing that eight days of people’s power can achieve more than eight years of progressive government”.
“Now begins real politics – politics which has always belonged to the people and which will return once and for all to the streets,” Racu added.
Housing is a sensitive issue in Spain because it impacts how people vote. The average rent in the country has almost doubled in the past 10 years. The average price per square metre has risen from 8.20 euros (about $9.08 at the time) in 2016 to 15.1 euros ($17.15) this year, according to the real estate website Idealista.
In an October 1 article for Vanity Fair, Spanish journalist Jorge Dioni Lopez noted that after eight years in power, housing is what is causing Sanchez to lose support among educated young people, children of the middle classes.
“The government faces a difficult situation. Firstly, because most of the relevant powers lie with other levels of government. Secondly, because Spain is a nation of homeowners. Unlike in other European countries, almost all the public housing that has been built has ended up in private hands, and historically, homeownership has been more attractive than renting,” he wrote.
“Housing is our savings system and also the primary means of transmitting wealth between generations,” he added.
After Spain’s parliament rejected the government’s housing reform bills on Friday, the leader of the centre-right People’s Party, (PP) Alberto Nunez Feijoo, urged Sanchez to “call elections as soon as possible”, claiming he had the support of “neither the streets nor parliament”.
While Sanchez has not officially announced new elections, the Spanish online news site El Diario has reported that elections could take place as early as November 29. Spain’s general elections are currently scheduled for July 2027.
