35,2026-10-04,"
  By AJ Eustace |
  at October 3, 2026 8:23pm CDT  
Six known players have elected minor league free agency in the past three days. While Major League free agents hit the open market five days after the end of the World Series, these eligible minor leaguers finished the season on outright assignments, so they’re able to get a head start on free agency. For further reference, these free agent decisions are all listed on the official MLB.com or MiLB.com transactions pages.
Pitchers
Jason Alexander (Astros)
The 33-year-old Alexander got recalled and optioned three separate times early in the season and eventually spent most of it with Triple-A Sugar Land. He had a 5.26 ERA in 87 1/3 innings there with a middling 18.5% strikeout rate. He did throw six scoreless innings for Houston on May 19th, but he allowed 19 earned runs in his other 12 1/3 innings this year. The Astros designated him for assignment in late September. Alexander has a 47.5% ground ball rate in parts of three seasons in the Majors, so he’ll latch on somewhere as a depth option.
Tanner Rainey (Tigers)
Formerly a staple of the Nationals’ bullpen, Rainey only tossed seven big league frames for the Tigers this year. He pitched 47 2/3 innings for Triple-A Toledo and was serviceable there, posting a 3.40 ERA with a 32.7% strikeout rate. Walks have been a problem throughout his career, and this year was no exception, as Rainey gave out free passes at a 14.1% clip in Toledo. Detroit designated him for assignment on August 31st and outrighted him two days later.
Corbin Martin (Cubs)
Martin joined the Cubs on a minor league deal in January. He was selected in mid-April but only appeared in seven games before getting DFA’d on May 7th. Working around three minor league IL stints, Martin pitched 24 ineffective innings for the Cubs’ Triple-A affiliate in Iowa, delivering an ERA over 6.00 and a walk rate of 16.7%. He typically posts ground ball rates in the low to mid-40s in the upper minors.
Outfielders
Will Benson (Reds)
Benson, 28, opened the season on the Reds’ active roster and stuck around in a part-time role through the middle of June. Over 114 plate appearances, he walked 14.0% of the time but struck out at a 33.3% clip that matched his career rate. Benson put up a 128 wRC+ in 2023 but hasn’t done anything near that since. He was outrighted to Triple-A Louisville on July 8th and batted .205/.295/.312 at that level overall.
Drew Waters (Royals)
Of the hitters on this list, Waters is the only one who did not appear in the Majors this year. He was a bit below average for Triple-A Omaha, batting .262/.323/.446 with 14 home runs and 21 stolen bases. Waters has an 84 wRC+ in 684 big league plate appearances from 2022-25. He’s a serviceable defender at the corner outfield spots but less so in center.
Infielders
Jonah Bride (Rangers)
Bride was released from Triple-A Round Rock in July, re-signed on a new minor league deal, and got selected for two games in August before getting DFA’d and outrighted. Bride put up a 125 wRC+ for the Marlins in 2024, but like Benson, he hasn’t done anything since. He got on base over 40% of the time with Round Rock, though, so it’s plausible that he’ll picked up as depth.

UK’s $1.6M second weekend at 554 sites ranked third, -22% for a cume of $5.5M. Spain did $1.1M second frame at 392 locations, No. 2, -25% for a local cume of $3.6M. Australia posted $1.1M No. 2 in second frame at 290, -17% for a total of $3.3M while Germany grossed $955K in second weekend at 428 theaters, No. 3, +2% for a cume of $2.3M. The Heart of the Beast storms into Korea on Oct. 29.

trimmer is good, I have it for 2 years now, still going strong, but the deal itself could be better , given so much dewalt things on sale last 12 months we could wait for better deals.
I noticed the other deals, but honestly I just needed a line trimmer. I looked at the DeWalt multi-tool kit (DCMAS571) from Sydney Tools and Total Tools for around $650, including the 8A fast charger, line trimmer attachment, and a bonus 54V 6Ah battery via redemption.
After reading a few reviews, I saw a common complaint about the powerhead handle getting in the way (some people even call it the ""nut cracker""), with a few owners modifying or trimming parts of it to make it more comfortable. Since I wanted a rock-solid line trimmer first and foremost, I decided not to risk dealing with potential returns or warranty headaches.
Realistically, I'll be using the line trimmer every other week through summer, so I figured it made more sense to prioritise something that does that job really well before worrying about multi-head attachments and other options later.
That said, if you don't already have DeWalt gear, there are some fantastic deals around at the moment. They just didn't really fit what I needed.

38,2026-10-04,"This is a Supreme x Coleman CT200U Minibike from 2017, it remains in as-new condition, and it’s being sold as new old stock as a result.
These Supreme-branded minibikes were released nine years ago as a limited edition and they’ve become hugely collectible in the years since.
The bike is finished in Supreme’s signature red, as they all were, it has large white Supreme logos on the fenders and seat, along with the “Ride or Die” graphic on the chain guard.
The Coleman CT200U is an old-school 200cc (actually 196cc) pull-start minibike, a throwback to the early days of the minibike craze when low-volume American manufacturers dominated the market, long before the Big Four Japanese motorcycle marques got involved and introduced true mass-production to the genre.
One of my favorite features of the CT200U is that the owner’s manual lists its front and rear suspension as “low pressure tires,” while technically true, it also shows just how simply these bikes are made.
The layout of the CT200U was directly influenced by the American minibikes of the 1960s, a time when when rigid-framed machines powered by lawnmower engines seemed to be almost everywhere, and most riders got their start on them.
Taco, founded by the off-road racer John Steen, was one of the best-known minibike makers of the time period. More sophisticated factory minis from Honda and Kawasaki, including the Z50 and the Coyote came later, and more of less pushed the local low-volume manufacturers like Rupp, Bonanza, Lil Indian, and Arctic Cat out of the market.
The Coleman CT200U is powered by an air-cooled, overhead-valve single-cylinder engine with a 68 mm bore, a 54 mm stroke, 8.5:1 compression and a recoil starter. Coleman rates it at 5.1 bhp at 3,600 rpm and 8.1 lb ft of torque at 2,500 rpm, although retail listings often describe the engine more aspirationally as producing 6.5 bhp.
Either way, it’s enough power to get you moving, but not enough to get yourself into serious trouble. Unless you’re exceptionally talented at finding trouble that is.
The Coleman CT200U is powered by an air-cooled, overhead-valve single-cylinder engine with a 68 mm bore, a 54 mm stroke, 8.5:1 compression and a recoil starter. Coleman rates it at 5.1 bhp at 3,600 rpm and 8.1 lb ft of torque at 2,500 rpm.
Power goes back through a centrifugal clutch and chain drive with a 10:1 gear ratio, so the rider has nothing to shift and no clutch to operate. The bike has one brake, a drum on the rear wheel, which is worked by a lever on the left handlebar. Having only a rear brake helps avoid new riders locking up the front and having a surprise dirt breakfast.
The bike rides on AT19x7-8 tires from the factory, inflated to a soft 10 psi, it has a 0.95 gallon fuel tank, gear racks front and rear, and it has a claimed top speed of 20 mph, with a curb weight of 121 lbs. It was officially recommended for riders aged 13 and older, however plenty of kids quite a bit younger than that have had the best summers of their lives tearing around a paddock on one.
Supreme announced a surprise collaboration with Coleman on Instagram in mid-2017 and two days later the bike went on sale (online only), as part of the brand’s Spring/Summer 2017 season. It was painted in Supreme’s box-logo red with black accents, with a Supreme logo on the fender and a branded seat.
The standard CT200U usually sold for $699.99, and Supreme’s version was priced at $998. It sold out so quickly that many who wanted one simply couldn’t get one. Their prices on the aftermarket quickly shot well into the mid-four-figures, and values have climbed higher since then.
This is an original 2017 Supreme x Coleman CT200U minibike, it’s an unused example of the limited-edition collaboration that was released as part of Supreme’s Spring/Summer 2017 collection.
The bike is finished in Supreme’s signature red, as they all were, it has large white Supreme logos on the fenders and seat, along with the “Ride or Die” graphic on the chain guard.
This is an original 2017 Supreme x Coleman CT200U minibike, it’s an unused example of the limited-edition collaboration that was released as part of Supreme’s Spring/Summer 2017 collection.
This particular example is described as new old stock as it’s reportedly remained factory-new and has never been started since leaving its original box. That makes it considerably more interesting as a collectible than a used example, particularly given how quickly the original online release sold out.
It’s now due to across the auction block with Bonhams on October the 11th with a price guide of €6,500 – €7,500 which works out to approximately $7,400 – $8,550 USD. If you’d like to read more about it or register to bid you can visit the listing here.
Images courtesy of Bonhams

Silodrome was founded by Ben back in 2010, in the years since the site has grown to become a world leader in the alternative and vintage motoring sector, with well over a million monthly readers from around the world and many hundreds of thousands of followers on social media.

41,2026-10-04,"By providing your information, you agree to our Terms of Use and our Privacy Policy.

“The History of Concrete” surpassed the $1 million mark at the box office on Saturday, becoming the highest-grossing platform-distributed documentary of 2026. It reached the milestone after just 16 days in theaters. 
Magnolia acquired “The History of Concrete” out of the Sundance Film Festival, where it attracted a great deal of coverage for its creator, John Wilson, the star of the cult favorite HBO series “How To with John Wilson.” The film examines urbanism and architecture and lives up to its title by chronicling the history of concrete in an absurdist fashion (Wilson tries to make the film in the style of a Hallmark movie).

 








Related Stories ‘A Statement’ Review: Tom McCarthy’s Talky Climate Crisis Seriocomedy Is Light Entertainment That Hardly Rakes the Muck Will ‘Behemoth!’ Push Pedro Pascal Into Oscar Contention? 
“The History of Concrete” grossed just over $450,000 from 160 screens over the weekend, putting its total at just north of $1.2 million. That’s a huge number for a documentary and a testament to the fan base that Wilson has developed. 
“The History of Concrete” already shattered records at the IFC Center, becoming its second highest-grossing debut behind “Parasite.” Last week, Magnolia saw strong numbers for the film in many markets, including earning $47,510 at the Music Box in Chicago, $34,534 at BAM in Brooklyn, and $24,653 at the Balboa, a small neighborhood theater in San Francisco. Low Cinema, a theater that Wilson operates in Ridgewood, Queens, has sold out every showing of “History of Concrete” through the end of October. Audiences at the arthouses were predominantly comprised of younger moviegoers, the same demographic driving cinemas’ comeback this year.

 









“The History of Concrete” has yet to match the grosses of other documentaries this year that debuted on many more screens. “Melania,” Brett Ratner’s penetrating exploration of the life of First Lady Melania Trump, packed in the MAGA faithful to the tune of $16.7 million. That would be a strong result had Amazon MGM Studios not shelled out $40 million to buy the film and tens of millions more to market it. The suspiciously large price tag suggested Jeff Bezos might have had more on his mind than just a love of nonfiction moviemaking. President Donald Trump later reported that he and his wife made $10.71 million in licensing fees from the deal. America, right? 
Neon also scored with “EPiC: Elvis Presley in Concert,” which opened across 325 IMAX theaters (not as a platform roll-out like “The History of Concrete”) and ultimately earned $23.5 million globally and $13.6 million domestically. In its second weekend, “EPiC” was playing in 1,963 theaters. “Melania” opened across 1,778 screens.
In terms of documentaries that were rolled out in a platform fashion instead of going wide first, Kino Lorber’s “Mary Oliver: Saved by the Beauty of the World” earned $773,430, the independently released “Steal This Story, Please!” netted $750,077, and Watermelon Pictures’ “American Doctor” earned $556,703.

 









The success of “The History of Concrete” is good news for Magnolia, which also had a surprise hit last summer with John Early’s directorial debut, “Maddie’s Secret.”

42,2026-10-04," Burry, who gained prominence for his bets against the U.S. housing market before the 2007â2009 financial crisis, wrote that new research led him to conclude that ""the bubble in AI may burst sooner than later,"" the report stated [1]. The investor said the change to put options gives him more cost-effective leverage over a shorter time horizon. ""Fundamentally, I am moving timelines up,"" Burry wrote in the newsletter, according to CNBC. ""As such, I want more leverage in my short positions. Better timelines make leverage more palatable. Nothing says leverage like options, in this case put options, which are relatively cheap due to exceptionally tight volatility measures such as the VIX"" [1]. Burry said some of the moves were intended to reduce his tax liability, but that most were driven by his view on the timing of an AI bubble. The repositioning suggests the AI trade could flip by next summer, according to the report [2]. Long-standing critics of the AI buildout have argued that the sector rests on speculative projections and cheap debt rather than real revenue, a view that has circulated across independent financial commentary for months [3][4].Burry Replaces AI Short Positions With Put Options Burry said he swapped his Micron short with puts at a June expiration in a $500 strike price range, according to the newsletter. He replaced his Nebius short with puts at the June expiration in a double-digit strike price range, the report stated. He also replaced his SOXX iShares Semiconductor ETF short position with September 2027 puts in the low $400s [1]. The investor said he ""replaced and rolled the Palantir short and put position into an enlarged put position"" centered at a September 2027 expiration in the low $100s, according to CNBC. Palantir has drawn repeated scrutiny from Burry, who has publicly questioned the company's valuation and claimed that AI startup Anthropic is effectively competing with its business model, the report stated [5]. Burry's previous positions were conventional short sales, which carry theoretically unlimited loss potential if a stock keeps rising. Put options, by contrast, cap the buyer's loss at the premium paid while still delivering leveraged downside exposure. Burry said the shift was mostly because he thinks ""the bubble in AI may burst sooner than later,"" according to the newsletter [1]. The Nasdaq Composite closed at a record the prior week, even as several individual AI-linked names remained below their highs, the report stated [1].Ares Report Cited on AI Capital Spending Assumption Burry cited recent research from Ares Management that emphasized reliance on unproven revenues in the AI space, structured with demanding legal agreements, according to the newsletter. The Ares report said the AI boom is relying on an assumption of ""sustained AI capital spending,"" the report stated [1]. The Ares report said it would take only a season in which AI revenue disappoints the capital expenditure underwriting it. ""In that scenario, a handful of boards, predisposed to redeploy capital toward the highest-conviction bet, would simply need to conclude that the highest-conviction bet has shifted. The legal documents contemplate that decision,"" the Ares report says, according to the newsletter [1]. Broader skepticism about the sustainability of the AI capital spending cycle has surfaced repeatedly in independent financial commentary, with critics pointing to circular financing arrangements and rising debt loads tied to data center construction [6][7]. Companies and utilities have continued to expand capacity, however. Caterpillar reported second-quarter revenue of $20.54 billion, up 24% from a year earlier, driven in part by the data center buildout, according to ZeroHedge [8].Acer CEO Comments on Memory Chip Cyclicality Burry also cited Acer CEO Jason Chen, who told a Taiwanese media outlet that cyclicality was due to return to the memory chip sector. Chen was quoted as saying China's production capacity has been consistently increasing and there is no shortage issue, according to the quote cited by Burry [1]. Chen said contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down, according to the quote cited by Burry. ""How could there be a continuous shortage? China's production capacity has been consistently increasing, and there is absolutely no shortage issue. Contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down,"" Chen was quoted as saying [1]. The comments bear on Micron, the U.S. memory manufacturer that Burry is now betting against through June puts. Reports have indicated that China's largest memory company, CXMT, is testing a pilot production line for next-generation bonded DRAM in Hefei, aiming to achieve high-performance memory without advanced EUV lithography, according to ZeroHedge [9]. The trend of expanding Chinese capacity forms part of the cyclical backdrop Chen described [9].References   CNBC. ""Michael Burry believes the AI bubble 'may burst' sooner than he first believed"". Contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down,"" Chen was quoted as saying [1]. The comments bear on Micron, the U.S. memory manufacturer that Burry is now betting against through June puts. Reports have indicated that China's largest memory company, CXMT, is testing a pilot production line for next-generation bonded DRAM in Hefei, aiming to achieve high-performance memory without advanced EUV lithography, according to ZeroHedge [9]. The trend of expanding Chinese capacity forms part of the cyclical backdrop Chen described [9].References   CNBC. ""Michael Burry believes the AI bubble 'may burst' sooner than he first believed"". ""Fundamentally, I am moving timelines up,"" Burry wrote in the newsletter, according to CNBC. ""As such, I want more leverage in my short positions. Better timelines make leverage more palatable. Nothing says leverage like options, in this case put options, which are relatively cheap due to exceptionally tight volatility measures such as the VIX"" [1]. Burry said some of the moves were intended to reduce his tax liability, but that most were driven by his view on the timing of an AI bubble. The repositioning suggests the AI trade could flip by next summer, according to the report [2]. Long-standing critics of the AI buildout have argued that the sector rests on speculative projections and cheap debt rather than real revenue, a view that has circulated across independent financial commentary for months [3][4].Burry Replaces AI Short Positions With Put Options Burry said he swapped his Micron short with puts at a June expiration in a $500 strike price range, according to the newsletter. He replaced his Nebius short with puts at the June expiration in a double-digit strike price range, the report stated. He also replaced his SOXX iShares Semiconductor ETF short position with September 2027 puts in the low $400s [1]. The investor said he ""replaced and rolled the Palantir short and put position into an enlarged put position"" centered at a September 2027 expiration in the low $100s, according to CNBC. Palantir has drawn repeated scrutiny from Burry, who has publicly questioned the company's valuation and claimed that AI startup Anthropic is effectively competing with its business model, the report stated [5]. Burry's previous positions were conventional short sales, which carry theoretically unlimited loss potential if a stock keeps rising. Put options, by contrast, cap the buyer's loss at the premium paid while still delivering leveraged downside exposure. Burry said the shift was mostly because he thinks ""the bubble in AI may burst sooner than later,"" according to the newsletter [1]. The Nasdaq Composite closed at a record the prior week, even as several individual AI-linked names remained below their highs, the report stated [1].Ares Report Cited on AI Capital Spending Assumption Burry cited recent research from Ares Management that emphasized reliance on unproven revenues in the AI space, structured with demanding legal agreements, according to the newsletter. The Ares report said the AI boom is relying on an assumption of ""sustained AI capital spending,"" the report stated [1]. The Ares report said it would take only a season in which AI revenue disappoints the capital expenditure underwriting it. ""In that scenario, a handful of boards, predisposed to redeploy capital toward the highest-conviction bet, would simply need to conclude that the highest-conviction bet has shifted. The legal documents contemplate that decision,"" the Ares report says, according to the newsletter [1]. Broader skepticism about the sustainability of the AI capital spending cycle has surfaced repeatedly in independent financial commentary, with critics pointing to circular financing arrangements and rising debt loads tied to data center construction [6][7]. Companies and utilities have continued to expand capacity, however. Caterpillar reported second-quarter revenue of $20.54 billion, up 24% from a year earlier, driven in part by the data center buildout, according to ZeroHedge [8].Acer CEO Comments on Memory Chip Cyclicality Burry also cited Acer CEO Jason Chen, who told a Taiwanese media outlet that cyclicality was due to return to the memory chip sector. Chen was quoted as saying China's production capacity has been consistently increasing and there is no shortage issue, according to the quote cited by Burry [1]. Chen said contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down, according to the quote cited by Burry. ""How could there be a continuous shortage? China's production capacity has been consistently increasing, and there is absolutely no shortage issue. Contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down,"" Chen was quoted as saying [1]. The comments bear on Micron, the U.S. memory manufacturer that Burry is now betting against through June puts. Reports have indicated that China's largest memory company, CXMT, is testing a pilot production line for next-generation bonded DRAM in Hefei, aiming to achieve high-performance memory without advanced EUV lithography, according to ZeroHedge [9]. The trend of expanding Chinese capacity forms part of the cyclical backdrop Chen described [9].References   CNBC. ""Michael Burry believes the AI bubble 'may burst' sooner than he first believed"". Caterpillar reported second-quarter revenue of $20.54 billion, up 24% from a year earlier, driven in part by the data center buildout, according to ZeroHedge [8].Acer CEO Comments on Memory Chip Cyclicality Burry also cited Acer CEO Jason Chen, who told a Taiwanese media outlet that cyclicality was due to return to the memory chip sector. Chen was quoted as saying China's production capacity has been consistently increasing and there is no shortage issue, according to the quote cited by Burry [1]. Chen said contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down, according to the quote cited by Burry. ""How could there be a continuous shortage? China's production capacity has been consistently increasing, and there is absolutely no shortage issue. Contract prices are currently fluctuating at a high level, with some prices going up and others down,"" Chen was quoted as saying [1]. The comments bear on Micron, the U.S. memory manufacturer that Burry is now betting against through June puts. Reports have indicated that China's largest memory company, CXMT, is testing a pilot production line for next-generation bonded DRAM in Hefei, aiming to achieve high-performance memory without advanced EUV lithography, according to ZeroHedge [9]. The trend of expanding Chinese capacity forms part of the cyclical backdrop Chen described [9].References   CNBC. ""Michael Burry believes the AI bubble 'may burst' sooner than he first believed"". The investor said he ""replaced and rolled the Palantir short and put position into an enlarged put position"" centered at a September 2027 expiration in the low $100s, according to CNBC. Palantir has drawn repeated scrutiny from Burry, who has publicly questioned the company's valuation and claimed that AI startup Anthropic is effectively competing with its business model, the report stated [5]. Burry's previous positions were conventional short sales, which carry theoretically unlimited loss potential if a stock keeps rising. Put options, by contrast, cap the buyer's loss at the premium paid while still delivering leveraged downside exposure. Burry said the shift was mostly because he thinks ""the bubble in AI may burst sooner than later,"" according to the newsletter [1]. The Nasdaq Composite closed at a record the prior week, even as several individual AI-linked names remained below their highs, the report stated [1].Ares Report Cited on AI Capital Spending Assumption Burry cited recent research from Ares Management that emphasized reliance on unproven revenues in the AI space, structured with demanding legal agreements, according to the newsletter. The Ares report said the AI boom is relying on an assumption of ""sustained AI capital spending,"" the report stated [1]. The Ares report said it would take only a season in which AI revenue disappoints the capital expenditure underwriting it. ""In that scenario, a handful of boards, predisposed to redeploy capital toward the highest-conviction bet, would simply need to conclude that the highest-conviction bet has shifted. The legal documents contemplate that decision,"" the Ares report says, according to the newsletter [1]. Broader skepticism about the sustainability of the AI capital spending cycle has surfaced repeatedly in independent financial commentary, with critics pointing to circular financing arrangements and rising debt loads tied to data center construction [6][7]. Good Morning,
A Russian drone strike in August destroyed 8 million books at a Kharkiv warehouse belonging to Ranok, a Ukrainian publisher, more than 85% of its stock, including 628,000 new schoolbooks. The government estimates 13 million Ukrainian books were destroyed this summer, a third of everything printed in the country this year (The Guardian).
The British Museum, which has the Bayeux Tapestry on loan from France until July 2027, will now run an audio-described tour after Yusuf Ali Osman, who has no sight in either eye, filed an Equality Act claim. The museum had said light limits on the tapestry meant self-guided visits took priority (BBC).
Robert Lazzarini, the sculptor known for distorted American symbols, has shown the work planned for the Venice Biennale’s US pavilion, which went to Alma Allen after the University of South Florida couldn’t raise $5 million. It’s at MassArt’s museum in Boston (The New York Times).
And David Fincher’s six-year Netflix deal is over; Mank cost $25 million and spent one day on the streamer’s top 10, while The Killer took $452,000 at the box office against a $175 million budget (The Verge). Lenny Pearce, an Australian DJ, is selling out North American venues with raves for children under 5, home by 8 p.m. (NPR).
Told on X that the AI generator Suno has “solved infinite personalized music,” a Salon writer recalls sorting college-radio mail by its RIYL stickers and finding Fleet Foxes that way (Salon).

Conor here: A recent example of the politics-finance scrum on the field is last week’s Protect College Sports Act passed by the Senate. Here’s Marc Edelman, a professor of law at Baruch College’s Zicklin School of Business, on what that bill does:
“It allows national rules of price fixing and wage fixing on athletes’ salaries, which likely will shift more of the revenues from college sports into the hands of college coaches, athletic directors and administrators, and away from the labor force.”
Back in March, the New York Times ran a piece on the “best-case scenarios” of private equity “converging” with college sports. Price fixing and wage fixing didn’t make the cut.
By Alex Volonte, a graduate research and teaching assistant in journalism studies at the University of Florida. Originally published at The Conversation.
It was around a year ago that the podcast “Pablo Torre Finds Out” released the first episode of what would become a 17-part series into alleged efforts by the Los Angeles Clippers to circumvent the NBA’s salary cap rules.
Since then, the podcast team has netted a Pulitzer Prize, a National Magazine Award and an Edward R. Murrow Award for its independent investigative reporting on the story.
On Sept. 2, 2026, the NBA released the findings of its own investigation into the scheme, concluding that the Clippers had helped star forward Kawhi Leonard secure endorsement deals with four companies that did business with the team – and had offered those companies business in return – in violation of the league’s salary cap rules.
The punishment amounted to one of the largest in American sports history and included the Clippers being fined US$30 million and stripped of five future first-round draft picks, with team owner Steve Ballmer receiving a one-year suspension.
Yet as “Pablo Torre Finds Out” began unwinding the Clippers’ efforts to circumvent the salary cap, legacy sports media outlets largely stood by and watched – when they didn’t outright dismiss the podcast’s work.
To me, this is a story that showcases the growing importance of independent journalism. But it’s also a story about the shortcomings of traditional sports media. As someone who studies how sports is becoming increasingly intertwined with political and corporate power, I can’t help but wonder if the sports media is up to the task.
The ‘Toy Department’ Gets Serious
In the heyday of newspapers, the sports page helped sell subscriptions. But it was often disparaged as the “toy department,” since it featured less “hard” or serious reporting.
The sports page’s bread and butter has long been game stories, injury reports, trade rumors and opinion columns. Sports radio perfected play-by-play commentary and talk shows that often involved debates with fans. Magazines ran in-depth profiles of individual players and long-form stories about epic playoff battles. Television centered on live game broadcasts, postgame highlights and weekly shows. Books went deeper, whether through biographies about the lives of coaches and athletes or narratives of memorable seasons that detailed the construction of championship squads.
Over the years, these formats coexisted to create a relatively balanced media diet. At times, they were even able to produce noteworthy investigative stories: Boston College basketball’s point-shaving scandal in the 1980s; the pervasiveness of performance-enhancing drug use in MLB during the 1990s and 2000s; and what the NFL knew about long-term brain damage linked to repeated head injuries in the 2010s.
But because the intermingling of sports with politics and finance now runs so deep, holding power to account often requires investigations that transcend regular sports reporting. The Athletic’s Adam Crafton, for example, spent months digging through host-city contracts and public records in order to show how FIFA had shifted World Cup costs onto U.S. taxpayers.
Yet Crafton’s reporting and research for The Athletic remains an exception; much of the watchdog journalism on the sports industry has to be taken up by outlets that aren’t sports-centric, yet have an investigative reporting infrastructure already in place.
For example, ProPublica was able to expose the tax-evasion practices of wealthy sports teams owners in 2021. And in April 2026, Wired revealed how Madison Square Garden, the home of the New York Knicks and New York Rangers, was secretly using facial recognition technology to identify people entering its venues and compile profiles of them.
The Digital Deluge
The changing media landscape in the digital age has created openings for podcasters like Torre. But it has also made it more difficult to distinguish journalistic rigor from public relations.
Fans can now get their sports news from a motley crew of formats and personalities, ranging from social media personalities and podcasters to YouTube channels and streamers.
Meanwhile, the line between sports leagues and the media outlets that cover them has blurred. In January 2026, ESPN bought the NFL’s official TV network. Almost all major franchises now feature in-house media teams that run their own stories and social media accounts to compete with traditional outlets. The owner of the Boston Red Sox, John W. Henry, also happens to own the New England Sports Network and Boston’s primary newspaper of record, the Boston Globe.
Athlete-owned media channels like PlayersTV might offer an interesting window into athletes’ lives and perspectives, but these formats will always have strong incentives to veer into self-promotion.
Vehicles for Investment
The fragmentation of traditional sports media has occurred alongside the growing financialization of sports.
By financialization of sports, I mean the ways in which sports teams, leagues and athletes are increasingly seen as financial assets and vehicles for investment, rather than cultural or sporting institutions.
Because U.S. professional sports leagues operate as tightly controlled, closed markets, they can determine who is allowed into teams and what size stakes they can hold. Private equity was only recently granted permission to start investing in pro sports leagues. But even then, across the five major North American leagues, private equity firms’ involvement has been capped at a maximum of 30% of noncontrolling ownership.
That hasn’t made investment any less attractive, as sports teams have tended to regularly outperform the stock market by several degrees of magnitude.
Take the NBA’s Los Angeles Lakers. In August 2026, Mark Walter sold the team for $12.5 billion, a record-breaking sum in the history of professional sports. The sale took place less than a year after he bought a controlling interest in the team for a then-record valuation of approximately $10 billion, meaning the team’s valuation had increased 25% in just nine months.
Going back even further, Jerry Jones bought the NFL’s Dallas Cowboys for $150 million in 1989; as of September 2026, the franchise is worth $17 billion – an inflation-adjusted return of roughly 4,155%.
For these reasons, institutional investors such as RedBird Capital Partners and Arctos Partners have entered the sports market in recent years. On Aug. 11, 2026, MLB’s New York Yankees announced a $2.6 billion financing deal with Apollo Global Management that includes an eventual 16% stake in the team and values the Yankees at more than $12 billion.

Parking Money in What’s Popular
It’s easy to see why investors are drawn to professional sports.
Live sports remain one of the few areas of American life with genuine mass appeal that transcends politics, race and class. Virtually all top broadcasts and most-watched shows every year are sporting events.
Beyond a financial return, wealthy investors and entities see valuable teams and leagues as a way to purchase credibility in the eyes of the public through what’s called “sportswashing,” or using the widespread popularity of sports to burnish their reputations.
But the new streams of capital have created opportunities for financial misconduct. The Miami-based investment firm 777 Partners built a portfolio of international soccer clubs as part of an ambitious expansion financed by its broader investment business. The firm unraveled in 2025 after prosecutors accused its founder and chief financial officer of concealing hundreds of millions of dollars in financial problems from lenders and investors, a case bolstered by the CFO’s guilty plea.
Meanwhile, the rise of sports betting, fantasy leagues and prediction markets has further turned sports into a vehicle for financial speculation. And billions of taxpayer dollars continue to subsidize massive stadium projects, despite limited economic benefits and weak public support.
Reforming the Approach
I think the unlikely success of an independent reporter like Pablo Torre can serve as a teachable moment for sports journalism.
In a forthcoming article for the International Journal of Sport Communication, sports media scholar Roxane Coche and I argue how sports journalists could embrace more of a watchdog, investigative role in their coverage.
We suggest that sports journalists could have basic economics training to put them in a better position to report on financial schemes, analyze U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filings or understand cryptocurrencies. And then there are basic watchdog journalism methods that could be integrated into their everyday reporting: surveying public records, mining documents and fact-checking statements.
Sports has never simply been about which team wins and which team loses on a given day. But it’s becoming impossible to ignore the ways in which politics, finance, fanbases and athletes are increasingly reshaping the sports landscape.
In just the past 12 months, Josh Kushner, whose brother is the son-in-law of President Donald Trump, bought a stake in the Los Angeles Lakers. NBA and MLB players have been caught up in gambling investigations, and FIFA has explored selling a stake in its commercial rights to private investors.
These are all sports stories that warrant closer scrutiny.
“We suggest that sports journalists could have basic economics training to put them in a better position to report on financial schemes, analyze U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filings or understand cryptocurrencies.”
NC readers, however, will know that such ‘basic economics training’ will merely fill the heads of sports journalists with rationales for the continued dominance of the ultra-rich.  Something like, say, forensic accounting would be more helpful.

I'm sure by now we're all familiar with Alexa, Amazon's voice assistant, even though we may not have used it if we're more dependent on Google Assistant/Gemini or Siri.
Alexa+ is just a smarter, more conversational version of the original — or, to use technical jargon, it's a generative AI model. While it can do everything the original Alexa could, it does more than answer specific questions or perform set tasks. It can remember your personal information and can take context into account, which is how it becomes more conversational when you ask it to do something for you.
The result is a more personalised assistant that delivers better outcomes and learns from your behaviour in order to make more relevant suggestions.
Alexa+ is available on nearly all the current Echo devices and is a free add-on for Prime members, but a subscription costs AU$29.99 a month for everyone else. Another reason to hold on to the AU$9.99p/m Prime subscription, isn't it?
Whether Alexa+ is worth the upgrade is up to you and which AI assistant you prefer. With Gemini just as popular, there's competition and, much like its counterparts, Alexa+ is better at understanding you and one of the reasons for that is because you can feed it information which it will retain. You can tell it what you like and don’t like, share facts and key dates such as birthdays and the types of food you enjoy. You can also share photos, messages, emails, documents and calendar events, and get Alexa+ to draw upon the information for use in its responses and suggestions.

SAN DIEGO, Oct. 4, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The law firm of Robbins Geller Rudman & Dowd LLP announces that purchasers of Honeywell Aerospace Inc. (NASDAQ: HONA) common stock on the open market between June 29, 2026 and September 1, 2026, inclusive (the ""Class Period""), have until November 23, 2026 to seek appointment as lead plaintiff of the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit.  Captioned Green v. Honeywell Aerospace Inc., No. 26-cv-06779 (D. Ariz.), the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit charges Honeywell Aerospace as well as certain of Honeywell Aerospace's top executives with violations of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934.
If you suffered substantial losses and wish to serve as lead plaintiff of the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit, please provide your information here:
https://www.rgrdlaw.com/cases-honeywell-aerospace-inc-class-action-lawsuit-hona.html 
You can also contact attorneys Ken Dolitsky or Michael Albert of Robbins Geller by calling 800/851-7783 or via e-mail at [email protected].
CASE ALLEGATIONS: Honeywell Aerospace manufactures and supplies aircraft components, avionics, engines, and systems for airframe manufacturing, commercial airline, military and defense, business aviation, and space markets, as well as other markets in the aerospace industry.
The Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit alleges that defendants throughout the Class Period made false and/or misleading statements and/or failed to disclose that: (i) a small percentage of Honeywell Aerospace's suppliers had a ""disproportionate impact"" on sales; (ii) those suppliers were suffering supply constraints; (iii) the foregoing was reasonably likely to have a material unfavorable impact on sales and profitability; (iv) Honeywell Aerospace was under investigation for potential violations of the False Claims Act for failing to comply with cybersecurity requirements for government contracts; and (v) as a result of the foregoing, defendants' positive statements about the Honeywell Aerospace's business, operations, and prospects were materially misleading and/or lacked a reasonable basis.
The Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit further alleges that on August 5, 2026, Honeywell Aerospace announced second quarter 2026 earnings, reporting that net income fell 70% year-over-year and adjusted earnings per share declined 32% year-over-year.  Further, Honeywell Aerospace slashed its full year 2026 guidance, including a reduction in expected year-over-year adjusted EBIT growth from 7%-10% to flat-to-3%.  On this news, the price of Honeywell Aerospace stock fell more than 23%.
Then, on September 1, 2026, the complaint alleges that the U.S. Department of Justice announced that Honeywell Aerospace agreed to ""Pay Over $2M to Settle False Claims Act Allegations of Failing to Comply with Cybersecurity Requirements in a U.S. Department of Defense Contract.""  On this news, the price of Honeywell Aerospace stock fell further.
THE LEAD PLAINTIFF PROCESS: The Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995 permits any investor who purchased or acquired Honeywell Aerospace common stock on the open market during the Class Period to seek appointment as lead plaintiff in the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit.  A lead plaintiff is generally the movant with the greatest financial interest in the relief sought by the putative class who is also typical and adequate of the putative class.  A lead plaintiff acts on behalf of all other class members in directing the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit.  The lead plaintiff can select a law firm of its choice to litigate the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit.  An investor's ability to share in any potential future recovery is not dependent upon serving as lead plaintiff of the Honeywell Aerospace class action lawsuit.

                             (2026) Cite this article


                            We’re sharing this article early to provide faster access to peer-reviewed, accepted research. It is citable and carries a permanent DOI. This version is subject to further edits and will be replaced automatically by the final Version of Record. All legal disclaimers apply.
                        
Immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs) have transformed treatment for non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), but resistance to these therapies is common. We report here results from a phase I trial combining intratumoral injection of a CCL21-expressing dendritic cell (CCL21-DC) vaccine with pembrolizumab in patients with advanced non-small cell lung cancer (ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT03546361). The primary objectives were to determine the maximum administered dose (MAD) in the dose escalation phase and the objective response rate (ORR) in the dose expansion. Eleven patients were enrolled in the dose escalation cohort, and 12 patients were treated in the dose expansion cohort at the MAD of 3 ×107 cells/injection. The trial did not meet its primary endpoint for ORR as no objective responses were observed, though 36.8% of patients achieved a best response of stable disease. The combination therapy was well tolerated. Post-hoc analyses of longitudinal biopsies revealed that the therapy increased tumor infiltration by CD4+ T cells and novel T cell clones. Disease stability was associated with increased T cell receptor diversity and greater expansion of novel clones following therapy. However, many novel clones failed to persist over time, and immunosuppressive myeloid signaling signatures were identified across time points. Our findings overall demonstrated that CCL21-DC vaccination combined with pembrolizumab can promote T cell infiltration, but this did not translate into meaningful clinical efficacy, potentially due to limited tumor specificity of the T cell response and an inability to fully reprogram the tumor microenvironment.
We thank patients and their families for their participation in this clinical trial. We thank the clinical research staff at UCLA, including Cindy Tong, Ashley Chung, Sandra Hernandez, James Chauv, Jackson Lind-LeBuffe, Christine Kivork, and Kimberly Kelly, as well as Elvira Liclican for administrative support at UCLA. We acknowledge the contributions of the UCLA Technology Center for Genomics and Bioinformatics for their assistance with scRNA-seq, and the UCLA Jonsson Comprehensive Cancer Center (JCCC) Flow Cytometry Core Facility (which is supported by National Institutes of Health award P30 CA016042 and by the JCCC) for their assistance in cell sorting.

The Indianapolis Colts didn't start well, but a timely injury got them back on track. Indianapolis knocked Washington Commanders backup quarterback Marcus Mariota out of Sunday's game early, opening the door for the Colts to coast to a 30-13 victory. 
When Mariota was in the game, however, it didn't look like things would go that way. The veteran led two scoring drives before leaving, setting his team up with a 6-0 lead. The Colts, meanwhile, looked lifeless on offense and couldn't seem to string together a decent drive. 
But Indianapolis would have plenty of chances to get their offense sorted out after the Commanders turned to third-string quarterback Athan Kaliakmanis the rest of the way. The rookie did produce two big shot plays, but ultimately struggled to lead Washington to scoring drives. 
With Washington's offense mostly stagnant, the Colts were content to lean on Jonathan Taylor and the run game. Taylor managed a score in the second quarter and then scored another quick touchdown in the third quarter after the Colts intercepted Kaliakmanis.
The rookie battled back on the next drive, finding wideout Treylon Burks for a long touchdown, giving Kaliakmanis his first career NFL touchdown. Kaliakmanis had one more big throw — a 48-yard strike to Dyami Brown — but was otherwise held in check the rest of the way.
With the Colts already up a score, Daniel Jones rushed in a touchdown early in the fourth quarter to effectively put the game away. The team added a field goal on its next drive to put the nail in the coffin. 
Facing massively long odds, Kaliakmanis tried to lead the Commanders on another scoring drive, but couldn't convert on a late fourth down. The Colts took over with the game firmly in hand, took a few knees and won the contest 30-13. 
Indianapolis improved to 2-2 with the win. The team will take on the Pittsburgh Steelers in Week 5. 
The Commanders, meanwhile, will need to make some tough decisions after being forced to turn to their third-string quarterback Sunday. Jayden Daniels may be pushed into action sooner than expected after dislocating his left elbow in Week 3.

54,2026-10-04,"Do androids dream of electric neeps?
As I throw open the windows on my first day in Nivalis, breathing in the city's neon-streaked air and watching the smog roil across the blackened sky, my government-mandated AI automata informs me the district is in lockdown and a serial killer's on the loose. Five hours later, I've become embroiled in a child trafficking plot (the good kind, apparently, seeing as I'm helping them get out); I've stumbled across an oil rig and befriended its city separatists; I've inadvertently struck up a romance with a CorpSec agent who's trying to shut down my noodle bar; and I've met a woman who writes new childhoods for condemned criminals, in the city's hope that implanting them will make 'em behave. All this and I've only grown a single onion. Nivalis Nights, it's fair to say, is not your average farm-life sim.
It is, though, kind of fascinating as it starts from a familiar point and begins building outward. It's also, for context, the follow-up to developer Ion Lands' enjoyable neo-noir delivery adventure Cloudpunk, taking that game's stylish dystopia and reimagining it as the setting for a sort of mash-up of Cyberpunk 2077, Stardew Valley, and Dave the Diver. It's an odd mix, certainly, but it works.
A lot of its early success is down to Nivalis itself. Cloudpunk's city was mostly designed to be viewed from afar, but Nivalis Nights plays out in first-person, demanding a world that can withstand closer scrutiny. And Ion Lands more than rises to the challenge. This Nivalis might still be a world built from jagged pixel-art edges, but it's a richly detailed, wonderfully evocative one (stubbornly wonky framerate aside); a chance to live your own little Blade Runner life, right down to the Vangelis-inspired soundtrack.
But you barely have a minute to admire the way the hazy neon light cuts through the smog on your first day in Nivalis before your automata Ava summons you to the city's culinary centre, Meridian Market. A short taxi ride later, amid the endless bustle and colour, you're the somewhat begrudging new owner of a noodle bar, its previous manager saddling you with all of its debt as a parting gift too. This run-down food stand with its solely al fresco dining set-up is now your problem, but you've at least inherited its cook. And so, across Nivalis Nights' first few days, a crash course in entrepreneurship begins.
That starts with a trip to the furniture shop for a couple of new dining tables and chairs. More space means more customers; more customers means more cash. But more customers also means dwindling ingredients, so it's off to the market you go. Meanwhile, your cook is splitting duties between the kitchen and front-of-house; taking orders, making orders, serving them up. But unfettered business growth is mandatory in this corporate-governed hellhole, and before long, you'll be bolstering your menu, upgrading your decor, and hiring new staff - better cooks, more waiters, cleaners, even managers to look after the day-to-day tasks - so that customers stay happy and the cash continues to flow. Soon, you'll be thinking about wages, schedules and staff morale; you'll inherit a downtown greenhouse where you can grow crops, make cheese, manufacture meat substitutes, and cut out the marketstall middle men. Perhaps you'll start a second business; maybe even open a nightclub too.
For all its cyberpunk ambience, Nivalis Nights' loop of expansion is a pretty familiar one. Yet while the money does seem to flow a little faster if I'm at the noodle bar pitching in, it's surprising how little time I'm apparently expected to be hands-on with the business stuff. Take a taxi to Lowtown, a skytrain to Eastern Residential, or even a boat to the Stacks, and Ion Lands seems determined to distract. Down at the docks, there's a sudden leftfield lurch into vaguely Dave the Diver territory once some grizzled sailorbot hands over his rusty old trawler, leaving me free to chart the city's churning waters in search of exotic fish or ancient plunder. Which, if you're wondering, is how I stumble upon that separatist oil rig.
And I certainly wasn't expecting to see so much story seeded throughout Nivalis. Early on, I meet an old woman convinced her dead husband's personality has been uploaded into a hooverbot, which begins a new side quest. I chase him down, and get to decide whether to keep his secret or rat him out. Several hours later, I've got a hacker on speeddial, a burgeoning friendship with Danny Danetti at his doughnut shop (although my new CorpSec agent ""pal"" wants me to put an end to that), and I've somehow agreed to train Spartacus Doom in the art of bare metal boxing. And that serial killer thing? I suspect our paths will soon cross.
At this point, I've amassed a veritable shopping list of side quests - so much so I've barely even contemplated a carrot in hours - all accompanied by fully voiced dialogue you can subtly nudge in different directions, stoking romance perhaps, or an air of general disdain. And I've not even mentioned the nighttime swerves into stealth if you choose to break curfew and embark on a more nefarious path. To, say, rob your competitors blind while they sleep.
Truthfully, I haven't really figured out how all these disparate bits fit together, and how Nivalis Nights will evolve as the tutorials die down and it settles into its more regular rhythm. Or even if it'll manage to remain compelling as noodle bar ownership makes way for the more extravagant demands of a full-on business empire - which appears to be the end goal. And there are some niggling issues: that untameable framerate for one, some odd bugs, and a UI that's a touch too fiddly, a touch tempermental, with overbearing overlays and waypoint markers that aren't exactly helpful. But even so, it's enormously refreshing to sink into a life sim that doesn't try and suffocate me with tweeness, and that doesn't leave me dreaming deliriously of turnips.
Yesterday, when I woke up for another day of entrepreneurial toil, storm clouds had gathered and the rain was ASMRing gently against my apartment window. Far below, crowds milled beneath translucent umbrellas and the rain-drenched streets had all but vanished in the mirrored neon glow. And as I splashed toward a flying taxi, Vangelis synths beginning to swell, I thought: for now at least, this weird little dystopia feels like home.

Ethereum layer-2 network Blast is shutting down after its operating costs exceeded the revenue generated by the chain.
In a Friday post on X, Blast said it sees no “credible path” to making the network economically sustainable and asked users to withdraw their assets to Ethereum mainnet: “Unfortunately, the economics of operating the chain no longer make sense.”Blast was founded by Tieshun “Pacman” Roquerre, the founder of NFT marketplace Blur, in November 2023 with native yield on Ether and stablecoins and a points program tied to an anticipated token airdrop. 
The strategy helped attract more than $2 billion in deposits before mainnet launched in February 2024.  Its DeFi total value locked has fallen by more than 98% since its June 2024 peak, according to DeFiLlama data.
Ethereum developers have scheduled the network’s next major upgrade, Glamsterdam, to activate on the Sepolia testnet on Oct. 6.
Sepolia node operators must update both their execution-layer and consensus-layer clients before the activation, the Ethereum Foundation said.
One of Glamsterdam’s changes is enshrined proposer-builder separation. It will move the handoff between specialized block builders and validators into Ethereum’s protocol, reducing reliance on outside middleware.
Glamsterdam will introduce block-level access lists, which record the accounts and storage locations used during each block, and will enable parallel processing by clients to speed up throughput.

Crypto suffered its worst month of the year for hacks and exploits in September, with two blockchain security firms estimating losses at more than $766 million.
PeckShield counted 55 major incidents resulting in $766.5 million in stolen funds, while CertiK recorded 97 incidents and estimated losses at $768.4 million.
The month included the $388 million Bitget hack and a $320 million hack of the Liquid Network. More than $270 million was later returned, according to reports.

Other crypto hacks in the month included Safe Wallet, DCENT, and Duelbits, which lost $7.8 million, $6 million and $5.9 million, respectively.
Largest incidents in September. Source: CertiK

On Monday, Tom Cruise gamely signed autographs and posed for pictures outside The Directors Village for the Los Angeles premiere of “Digger,” flashing the confident smile that had made him a screen legend. 
Yet, long before Cruise hit the red carpet alongside Warner Bros. film chiefs Michael De Luca and Pamela Abdy, who he praised for greenlighting “Digger” without needing to turn a page of its script, it was clear that the movie was going to flop. It was too dark, too weird, too critically excoriated. Opening weekend projections kept getting revised downward no matter what Cruise did to hawk the satire — dancing through Jennifer Hudson’s “spirit tunnel,” explaining the finer points of VistaVision to the Kelce brothers, or talking about aging in a completely normal way with GQ.

 








Related Stories Box Office: John Wilson’s ‘The History of Concrete’ from Magnolia Passes $1 Million ‘A Statement’ Review: Tom McCarthy’s Talky Climate Crisis Seriocomedy Is Light Entertainment That Hardly Rakes the Muck 
But few could have anticipated just how badly the film would bomb. Well, “Digger” is DOA, having opened to a disastrous $8 million this weekend. It debuted far below the weekend’s other new release, “Verity,” an adaptation of a Colleen Hoover novel that critics have lambasted en route to its $32.6 million opening, and was also outgrossed by holdovers like “Resident Evil” and “Primetime.” International audiences, which typically show up for Cruise flicks, aren’t buying tickets. “Digger” earned $12 million from 74 markets to bring its global haul to a pallid $20 million. The only hope is that “Digger” can reemerge as an awards contender: an improbable outcome, but one that would provide some cultural value.

 









As it stands “Digger,” which officially cost $125 million to produce (sources peg the true price tag at between $160 million to $180 million), needs to make roughly $350 million theatrically to break even. Warner Bros., which backed the film with Legendary, has put the breakeven point at $300 million. That means “Digger” is staring at a loss of $150 million or more, according to two executives with knowledge of its finances. For context, theaters keep half of ticket sales and Warner Bros. spent tens of millions to market “Digger.” A spokesperson for Warner Bros. declined to comment. 
There’s not much to spin. “Digger” will go down as one of the biggest, if not the biggest flops of Cruise’s career. He’s had a few — “The Mummy,” for instance, lost $95 million and killed Universal’s Dark Universe ambitions in their cradle. The most recent Cruise film to open softer than “Digger,” the 2007 political thriller “Lions for Lambs,” was another disappointment, though it only cost $35 million to make. “Digger” is already a historic bomb, keeping company with the likes of “John Carter,” “Cutthroat Island,” and “The Lone Ranger.”
As for De Luca and Abdy, they’re still on the Warners lot, but they won’t be for long. The studio’s new owner, David Ellison, let them know Friday that they wouldn’t continue in their roles when Skydance assumes control and merges the company with Paramount. And the film’s massive losses will justify a pivot that felt inevitable as soon as word broke that Ellison was making a play for Warner Bros. — fewer “Diggers,” more “Days of Thunder 2’s.”

 









Sadly, the mogul may be right from a financial perspective. De Luca and Abdy were the most auteur-friendly of studio chiefs, giving Paul Thomas Anderson, Maggie Gyllenhaal, Ryan Coogler, and Alejandro González Iñárritu the kind of budgets they would have struggled to secure elsewhere.
Sometimes their bets paid off. Few would question the wisdom of the “Sinners” deal they made with Coogler returning the film’s copyright to him, though they did initially. After all, “Sinners” was a huge hit. More recently, De Luca and Abdy’s ambitious gambits have failed to reap profits. Gyllenhaal’s “The Bride!” and Iñárritu’s “Digger” are among the decade’s biggest money losers, while Anderson’s “One Battle After Another” was an Oscar-winning write-down. When “One Battle After Another” swept the Academy Awards, a Paramount executive reminded me of their different mandate. “We don’t lose $100 million to win Best Picture,” the exec scoffed.
That may be good news for Skydance’s shareholders, who are understandably worried about how Ellison is going to operate two entertainment giants while paying down $80 billion in debt, but it won’t be great for cinephiles. Love it or hate it, “Digger” is a bold swing. Iñárritu’s dark look at corporate greed and climate change wrestles with lots of big ideas, and Cruise gives the kind of go-for-broke performance that he largely abandoned to play an action hero. It may be a while before moviegoers see him in this mode again.

 









As for Cruise, he may have praised De Luca and Abdy’s chutzpah, but he also is very familiar with Ellison, having made “Top Gun: Maverick” and several “Mission: Impossible” films with Skydance. He’s already given the merger his blessing, telling Pat McAfee that bringing together Warner Bros. and Paramount will lead to “abundance.”
“They’re going to deliver 30 movies. It’s a community to me. It’s not an industry. The people in these studios are not just people in the studios, they’re my family,” Cruise told McAfee. “I know we’re going to get those 30 movies. I want us all to come together as a community to help make those 30 movies. It’s not just going to be Paramount doing it. Everyone’s going to need all the help of every artist to be able to do it.”
According to a report in TheWrap, when Abdy and De Luca got the call from Ellison telling them that they were out of the family, they had a tart response.
“Good luck to you,” Abdy told Ellison with some bitterness. “I left you 39 movies.”
But is it a slate filled with more “Sinners” than “Diggers”?

60,2026-10-04,"Foreign investors bought $358 million worth of Turkish stocks in the week ending Sept 25.
Recent turmoil among investment funds is expected to drive investors toward safer assets as they take a more cautious stance, an expert noted.
Investors had recently begun diversifying their portfolios across different investment instruments, but would now be much more cautious when making decisions, said Zeynel Balcı from Meksa Asset Management.
He said recent developments would, at least for a while, bring safe-haven assets to the forefront.
On Sept. 30, the Capital Markets Board (SPK) approved interim payments of up to 1 million Turkish Liras per eligible investor in funds established by Tera, Pusula, Atlas and Hedef portfolio management companies that are undergoing liquidation. According to the SPK, 131 investment funds designated for liquidation hold approximately 826 billion liras in assets across 455,758 investors.
Rebuilding confidence in the stock market would take time, said Balcı, adding that trust would gradually be restored through the implementation of necessary measures and the healthy functioning of markets.
The Fund Coordination Board, chaired by Vice President Cevdet Yılmaz, held its second meeting on Oct. 2 to discuss the payment schedule for fund accounts. The board assessed secondary regulations and the payment timetable related to the SPK’s decision on payments, according to the statement released after the meeting.
While describing the latest incident as serious, Balcı noted that similar confidence-shaking events had occurred in Borsa Istanbul in the past and that conditions eventually stabilized. He said investors would be more cautious for some time and would proceed with greater care.
Balcı said a shift toward deposits had already begun, noting that bank deposits, including participation funds, increased by nearly 1 trillion Turkish liras between Sept. 11 and Sept. 29.
According to Balcı, interest in foreign exchange remains limited. He also said there were few signs of a move toward gold and added that reports and market observations in recent days pointed to some gold sales linked to liquidity pressures.
Investors may be selling other investment instruments to meet cash needs and mandatory payments, said Balcı, suggesting that t some consumers could also postpone spending plans.
Balcı said the stock market had already reacted negatively to the developments, declining 20 percent from its peak and approaching levels commonly associated with a bear market.
He added that the selling pressure was not limited to the funds directly involved in the controversy but had spread to other stocks as well. According to Balcı, the market value of freely traded shares has fallen by trillions of Turkish liras.
Balcı also said that if equity-heavy private pension funds are taken into account, it is possible to speak of broader long-term damage stemming from the recent developments.

63,2026-10-04,"Home Design Ideas For Modern Living
Flea markets are packed with pieces that make little sense until you picture them somewhere else. Old utility carts, factory lights, battered cabinets, and furniture with worn finishes can look past their prime while hiding the exact details that make them worth bringing home.

Best finds are not always the pieces in perfect condition. Solid wood, iron frames, intact hardware, unusual proportions, and construction that would cost far more to reproduce can matter more than scratches or chipped paint.
Before walking away, these are the finds worth taking a second look at.
Table of Contents

Old utility carts are worth checking when the frame is solid and the wheels still move. This one pairs an iron frame with two thick wood shelves and raised rails that keep objects from sliding off.
Its proportions make it useful beyond its original job. Bottles can go on one level and glasses on another, or the cart can hold cookware, plants, books, or coffee supplies. Unlike a fixed cabinet, it can move when the room needs to change.

A bicycle missing its road-going future can still have value if the frame, wheels, and mechanical parts remain intact. Here, an entire bicycle supports a long wood counter, with the wheels and chain becoming part of the furniture.
It is an extreme example, but it makes a good case for looking beyond whether an old bike can still be ridden. Complete frames can become console bases, bar supports, shop displays, or wall installations without hiding what they once were.

Old industrial lights often look rough beside polished new fixtures, which is part of their appeal. Galvanized metal, rust, cages, and oversized shades bring texture that would be hard to reproduce with paint.
Check the shade and housing rather than the old wiring. Electrical components can be replaced by a qualified professional while the visible shell remains intact. Large factory shades work well over kitchen islands and dining tables where their scale has room to register.

Old spotlights and studio lamps stand apart from standard table lamps because the housing becomes as important as the light itself. This one combines a polished rectangular lamp body with a wood back and tripod-style support.
Look for intact housings, adjustment hardware, handles, and mounting points. Even when the original electrical parts are unusable, the shell may be worth restoring and rewiring.

Don’t dismiss a heavy old case because you have no reason to travel with it. Rigid trunks and suitcases can hide useful storage, and their hinges, handles, corners, and interior compartments give them details most cabinets lack.
The case shown here opens into a compact bar with bottle racks, shelves, glass storage, and drawers. When shopping, test the hinges and inspect the shell for structural damage; worn surfaces matter less if the case still opens and closes as it should.

Look twice at cabinets built around removable wood bins. This one combines four deep box-style compartments with three shallow center drawers, giving it the kind of mixed storage that is hard to find in a standard chest.
The open hand slots mean the large bins can be pulled out instead of searched from above. That setup could work for toys, linens, shoes, craft supplies, pet gear, or anything bulky enough to overwhelm a drawer. Dark framing around the warm wood fronts also gives the cabinet enough contrast that scratches and worn edges don’t need to be disguised.

Cowhide can make a flea-market chair worth a second look before you even consider refinishing it. This butterfly chair uses one hide stretched across a thin black metal frame, with the brown-and-white markings left as the main feature. Thick leather lacing follows the perimeter and joins the sections across the seat and back.
Check the hide for cracks, tears around the attachment points, and dried edges. On a chair like this, an intact cover is a large part of the find. The slim frame takes up little visual space, while the natural pattern gives each chair a look that cannot be duplicated with printed upholstery.

Solid wood rounds are the part to notice here. Thick cross-sections form the seats and tabletop, preserving the irregular outer edge and uninterrupted grain rather than cutting the wood into uniform circles.
Flea-market stools with a dated or damaged base can still be worth buying when the top is a substantial piece of wood. Check for deep splits, rot, or loose sections rather than rejecting scratches and finish wear. A sound slab can be sanded and refinished, or moved onto another base if the original one does not suit your home.

Tall glass cabinets can solve a storage problem that wide dressers and bookcases cannot. These two use narrow wood frames, glass-front doors, interior shelves, and a distressed red finish that exposes darker layers around the edges.
Look at the glass, hinges, shelves, and frame before judging chipped paint. A sound cabinet can hold glassware, books, dishes, collectibles, or bathroom supplies while using little floor space. Here, stripping away every worn patch would also remove much of what distinguishes the cabinets from new reproductions.

This shelving unit has more going on than a standard open bookcase. An angled metal frame surrounds three wood display shelves, while a long distressed red wood tray occupies the lower level.
That combination gives you two types of storage in one piece. Open shelves can hold dishes, books, plants, or ceramics, while the tray can contain smaller items that would look scattered on an exposed shelf. At a flea market, check whether the metal frame sits square and whether the wood shelves have warped; worn paint and surface marks can stay.

This chair is worth separating from the butterfly chair because its construction is much more substantial. A black metal frame supports a cowhide seat and tall back, with brown leather side panels, exposed stitching, straight metal arms, and finial-like posts rising above the back.
Look at the seams where the hide wraps around the frame. Those stitched edges are part of the chair’s character but also the places most likely to show stress. If the frame and hide are sound, the mix of iron, leather, and cowhide already provides the contrast that people often try to add to a room through several separate pieces.

A tall shelf with a small footprint can be a better flea-market buy than another wide cabinet. This piece combines a dark metal frame with four wood shelves, each level narrowing toward the top.
The photo also shows one use that suits its proportions: a home bar. Bottles and ice buckets occupy separate levels instead of requiring a full bar cabinet. The same frame could hold plants, towels, cookware, or books. When shopping secondhand, check the joints and feet first; scratched wood or aged metal can remain without affecting how the piece works.

66,2026-10-04,"UNIQLO Market Street celebrates its grand opening with a free street festival (Oct 9) on Ellis Street between Stockton and Powell. The event includes DJ sets on two stages, interactive art and science activities, street soccer, Japanese calligraphy and origami workshops, food vendors, giveaways, and six large-scale Market Street art cubes by Bay Area artists. The festival is family-friendly and open to the public, with optional RSVP.
UNIQLO’s new Downtown SF location is at 801 Market Street, the old “Old Navy” location, a 1907 building at Market and 4th. UNIQLO previously had a flagship store in Union Square which closed down in March 2021. So a little more than 5 years later, UNIQLO is back in downtown.
Plus there are deals, performances and giveaways all weekend at the store.
Be part of a day filled with music, culture, creativity, and community at the UNIQLO Market Street Grand Opening Festival.
Celebrate the city. Join us for a day of music, culture, creativity, and community for the grand opening of UNIQLO Market Street. Experience local entertainment, special activations, giveaways, and opening-week exclusives. This event is free and family-friendly!
MOST UP TO DATE DETAILS HERE:
https://uniqlo.streetfest.us/
Lineup: The festival brings together more than 30 Bay Area artists, musicians, cultural institutions, and neighborhood businesses across Market, Ellis, and Powell streets, turning downtown into a showcase of local art, music, food, and community.
Ellis Street Stage
Powell Street Stage (Presented with theUnion Square Alliance)
Beyond the music and Market Street Art Cubes, explore interactive booths and workshops from our community partners:
Play – Street Soccer:
Street Soccer USA activates two pop-up street soccer pitches with community games and open play that promote social inclusion through sport. (Please wear appropriate shoes for street soccer on asphalt. Special giveaways and prizes are available.)
Make – Free Workshops and Interactive Art:
Collect – Festival Passport and Art Prints:
Featured Art Installations:
Getting Here:
Festival entry is at Ellis St. & Powell or Ellis St. & Stockton, one block from Powell Street BART and the cable car turnaround. We recommend taking public transportation or a rideshare. If driving, the nearest parking garages are at 5th & Mission, Union Square, and Ellis-O’Farrell.
Good to Know:
https://uniqlo.streetfest.us/
Disclaimer: Please double check event information with the event organizer as events can be canceled, details can change after they are added to our calendar, and errors do occur.

68,2026-10-04," DATEM (E472e) is a synthetic emulsifier widely used in industrial baking and processed foods to strengthen dough, increase loaf volume, improve texture and extend shelf life.  It is classified as ""Generally Recognized as Safe"" (GRAS) by the FDA, a historical designation that exempted it from modern pre-market approval and prioritized industrial utility over long-term health studies.  Independent research suggests DATEM may harm gut health by potentially compromising the intestinal lining (""leaky gut"") and disrupting the microbiome, which can lead to systemic inflammation.  This inflammation may aggravate digestive issues, autoimmune conditions and metabolic disorders, though DATEM causes disruption rather than a nutritional deficiency.  To avoid DATEM, consumers should read labels carefully, as it is common in commercial baked goods and packaged snacks and opt for whole foods or products with simple ingredient lists. 
 It is classified as ""Generally Recognized as Safe"" (GRAS) by the FDA, a historical designation that exempted it from modern pre-market approval and prioritized industrial utility over long-term health studies.  Independent research suggests DATEM may harm gut health by potentially compromising the intestinal lining (""leaky gut"") and disrupting the microbiome, which can lead to systemic inflammation.  This inflammation may aggravate digestive issues, autoimmune conditions and metabolic disorders, though DATEM causes disruption rather than a nutritional deficiency.  To avoid DATEM, consumers should read labels carefully, as it is common in commercial baked goods and packaged snacks and opt for whole foods or products with simple ingredient lists.

This inflammation may aggravate digestive issues, autoimmune conditions and metabolic disorders, though DATEM causes disruption rather than a nutritional deficiency.  To avoid DATEM, consumers should read labels carefully, as it is common in commercial baked goods and packaged snacks and opt for whole foods or products with simple ingredient lists.
