89,2026-10-04,"...
Odds are the Bucks won't be competing for the postseason this year. And there won't be any reason to keep tabs on the draft either since the Bucks are sending their pick to the Pelicans. Which means this year will be entirely about the players currently on the roster in the first season of the post-Giannis era.
Acquiring a pile of draft picks, plus a pile of young players, is what made the trade return from the Heat for Antetokounmpo so appealing. Tyler Herro is 26. Jaime Jaquez is 25. Kel'el Ware is 22. Kasparas Jakučionis is 20. On top of all the youth Milwaukee already had, the front office is in a position where it can evaluate the entire roster and decide who's a keeper.
Join or create a Yahoo Fantasy Basketball league for the 2026-27 NBA season
How the youth develops is obviously the most important storyline to monitor in Milwaukee this season: Will Brayden Burries or Nate Ament look like a future star? Could Ryan Rollins take another leap? How will all the pieces from Miami look?
But whether the Bucks' front office can turn players into assets matters too. And already front-office executives around the league are chirping about how the Bucks are a team that could be targeted because they still have something a lot of teams wish they had: Big men who can shoot 3s and protect the rim.
(Jonathan Castro/Yahoo Sports)
Ware and Myles Turner could help Milwaukee figure out what comes next, or they could help somebody else win a championship.
Ware never really earned the trust of Heat head coach Erik Spoelstra, so it'll be fascinating to see what he does with a longer leash under new head coach Taylor Jenkins. On one hand, his raw numbers are amazing: Ware averaged 11.1 points, 9 boards and 1.1 blocks in only 22.1 minutes while shooting nearly 40% from 3. But his defensive effort and focus came and went, so Spo never could empower him with more consistent minutes. But if he starts defending with more consistency and keeps making 3s, why wouldn't Milwaukee want to keep him?
Whereas, Turner is 30 years old and a proven commodity who can play with pace, drain 3s and block shots. He's just one year removed from being a pivotal piece on a Finals team. It's more realistic that the Bucks will be motivated to move Turner, not Ware. But there's nothing stopping Milwaukee from entertaining offers for both of them, or any of the players on the roster during this transitional year.
For Turner or Ware, I would argue that seven of the top 13 teams in my power rankings should have interest: the Celtics, Knicks, Lakers, Pistons, Raptors, Sixers and Timberwolves. If teams like the Bulls, Magic and Mavericks end up being competitive, then Turner or Ware could make sense for them too. That's 10 teams!
As for the Bucks: I put them 28th in my power rankings. Some fans took issue with that spot. Since the team has no incentive to be in the lottery, there's no reason to lose. And since they have a bunch of good young players, then the team could end up exceeding expectations. So maybe that's true. And you know what? Fair.
The trade deadline is months away. Right now, the Bucks need to find out what they actually have across the roster, and in particular with those two bigs. Can Jenkins get more consistent effort out of Ware? Can Ware and Turner share the floor together, giving Milwaukee two rim protectors without sacrificing spacing? And how much could Turner's veteran presence help all the young guards develop?
As much as fans of other teams might look to Milwaukee for trade machine fantasies this season, the Bucks also will see value in keeping what they have. The Bucks already made the trade that changed the direction of the franchise. This season should help them figure out their next one."

        A truckload of lumber passed me on the road the other day, and naturally, the analyst in me started thinking: there’s a Seeking Alpha article in that truck.Thinking about the truck, most peopleThis article was written byAnalyst’s Disclosure: I/we have no stock, option or similar…

91,2026-10-04,"Frankie Llerandi, 21, has been unable to land a single internship in his three years at Michigan State University.
He will graduate in the spring with an economics degree and an interest in data analytics, but true adulthood seems maddeningly far away. Most of his cousins have not been able to buy a house or start a family deep into their 30s.
Kira Saiti, a 19-year-old sophomore, is more upset about the broader world than her own future. Every time she looks at her phone she sees images of violence and horror, both in the Middle East and closer to home.
“It’s kind of hard to look at that and say, ‘This world is a great place,’” she said.
Julian Bautista, 20, saw his chosen profession of environmental workdecimated by Trump administration cuts, pushing the junior to decide he will leave the United States to find work in the Netherlands when he graduates. He considers what remains his home country for the next 18 months to be a failure of democracy led by what he sees as corrupt politicians.
“Throughout my life, we’ve either stagnated or declined, we haven’t improved,” Bautista said.
For many of their classmates on campus and elsewhere around the country, the present looks bleak and the future uncertain at best. Their anxiety is fueling a restlessness with a national political system that they worry is no longer capable of improving their lives or no longer interested in helping.
Two years ago, grim economic vibes helped Donald Trump win a far larger share of young voters than is typical for a Republican — 47%, according to AP VoteCast, an extensive survey of the electorate. Now, anxiety about artificial intelligence and the Iran war has been added to the mix, powering a wave of disruptive candidates on the opposite end of the political spectrum who have been embraced by college students.
Llerandi, Saiti and Bautista all support a slate of progressive, antiestablishment Democratic candidates in Michigan who are pledging to shake up the status quo. Among them are Abdul El-Sayed, a former public health official running for U.S. Senate, and Will Lawrence, a liberal activist trying to unseat Tom Barrett, the Republican congressman who represents the swing district that includes the university.
Anxieties about the job market, homeownership and adulthood are widespread across the campus, even among those who are not deeply involved in politics.
“It’s just something to laugh at, but, you know, I really do want a house when I’m older,” said Praveen Amunugama, a 21-year-old biochemistry major who plans to go to graduate school in hopes the job market improves over the next two years.
His friends feel the same way.
“They want to be able to afford things, raise a good family,” he said.
Despite the economic angst among young people, at first blush the picture does not look that bad for them. The unemployment rate for those age 22 to 27 is 7.2%, higher than the broader rate of 4.1% but lower than some other moments of economic calm.
A 2024 study found members of the younger generation known as Generation Z are poised to be wealthier than millennials, who themselves are wealthier than the prior generation despite the persistent sense of economic doom that accompanied their entry into the job market during the Great Recession.
Kevin Corinth, the economist who co-authored that study, said he understands Gen Z angst, given modern uncertainties, especially the prospect of AI reshaping various industries and data showing younger people are taking longer to leave home and start families.
“For previous generations, if you took certain paths you were likely to succeed, to do better than your parents,” said Corinth, who is at the American Enterprise Institute, a center-right think tank. For young people now, he acknowledged, that path is not as clear.
Kathryn Edwards, a Texas-based economist, noted that unemployment among recent college graduates is at 5.6%, higher than at most other times in recent history other than the Great Recession. Combined with historically high housing prices and rising costs for essentials such as foodand healthcare, that is a challenging environment for young people.
“It’s pretty bad for them,” Edwards said, adding that young people are picking up on broader warning signs in the economy. “So many aspects of our current economy, I think young people have gotten right.”
They are also fully exposed to the new form of job hunting: AI-powered, digital and done almost exclusively online, where it is hard to discern real job openings from fake ones.
“Think about the amount they get spammed and scammed, and there are tons of fake jobs out there,” said Marla McGraw, assistant dean of career management in Michigan State’s business school. “They’re always coming in here going, ‘Is this real?’”
She spends a lot of time trying to buck up disenchanted students.
“Our message right now is the job market is tough, but you’re tougher,” McGraw said.
Young Americans’ optimism about the job market has plummeted since 2024 and the gap between their view of the economy and those of their elders is greater than almost anywhere in the world, according to a Gallup survey earlier this year.
They are less likely to be engaged in politics and less likely to think it is important to vote, an Associated Press-NORC Center for Public Affairs Research poll found. They also are less likely to be concerned about political violence.
That dissolution of beliefs in traditional political values worries Karlee VanAntwerp.
The 21-year-old Michigan State senior is also head of the local chapter of Turning Point, the group for young conservatives whose founder, Charlie Kirk, was assassinated last year. VanAntwerp has been horrified to see Kirk’s killing repeatedly, and sometimes menacingly, mocked online.
“Gen Z tends to be more extreme with their views,” VanAntwerp said. “It makes it hard to work with people of different political beliefs, because even if you’re in the middle, they assume you’re crazy.”
VanAntwerp sees that feeding into the despair many of her generation feel about real change ever happening.
“One administration does a bunch of stuff, another administration comes in and wipes it all out,” said VanAntwerp, who is also working for Barrett’s campaign. “It feels like we always go in circles. That contributes to the political temperature being so high, because people feel nothing changes.”
Her politics are the polar opposite of many liberal students who gathered to hear Lawrence, Barrett’s Democratic challenger, talk in a classroom one recent evening, but there was a similar desire for dramatic change. Lawrence sympathized with the glum feelings of early adulthood and urged the students not to succumb to despair.
“When I was a little younger I wanted to drop out of society, and it was organizing that gave me a path in life,” Lawrence said.
After the candidate left, however, Bautista still sounded on the verge of despair. He talked about how online influencers he trusts deride Democrats such as Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer of New York as “mini-mes” because, Bautista contended, they are just watered-down versions of Trump.
“Our leaders are representing us less,” Bautista said. “People are desperate for big stuff.”

94,2026-10-04," News
BMW is cleaning house, but it's also preparing a wave of new models to replace some of the ones on their way out.
   Adrian Padeanu  
 October 4, 2026
BMWBLOG » News » Which Cars Do You Think BMW Is Going To Axe?
BMW has finally confirmed what we already suspected: the 2 Series Active Tourer is going away. It won’t happen today or tomorrow, but Munich won’t have a third generation of its compact minivan. More importantly, the Active Tourer won’t be the only model heading for the automotive graveyard. Days ago, the company confirmed it’s cutting models as part of a broader effort to slash costs and improve profitability.
BMW hasn’t provided a complete list of the casualties, though, leaving us to play detective. Regardless of which models get the axe, BMW won’t discontinue them overnight. Instead, BMW will phase them out once the current-generation models run their course. We’ve already said goodbye to the X4, Z4, and 8 Series, and several other models look increasingly vulnerable.
So, which BMWs do we think are next?
Let’s start with the only one BMW has actually confirmed. The 2 Series Active Tourer will not get a third generation, ending an unusual chapter in BMW history. The original model arrived in 2014 as the firm’s first front-wheel-drive vehicle, breaking one of the brand’s longest-standing traditions in the process.
The current generation followed in 2021, but apparently wasn’t profitable enough to justify another go-around. According to insiders, the Bavarian people-mover will be retired in 2029, but nothing’s official yet.

This one hasn’t been confirmed, but the 4 Series Gran Coupe looks like another strong candidate for the chopping block. The current model is built in Munich alongside the i4, and that’s where things get interesting. BMW has spent around €650 million transforming its Munich plant into an EV-only facility. From 2027 onward, the factory will produce exclusively fully electric vehicles. The i3/M3 are among the models it will build there, while the combustion-engined 3 Series is moving to Dingolfing.
That creates a pretty obvious question: What happens to the gas-fueled 4 Series Gran Coupe? BMW could theoretically move production to Dingolfing, where it builds the Coupe and Convertible, but that’s a lot of effort for a model that already occupies a slightly awkward spot in the lineup. The 4 Series Coupe and Convertible are expected to soldier on until 2029, but the Gran Coupe may not be so lucky.
As with the combustion-engined 4 Series Gran Coupe, its electric counterpart is made at the company’s oldest plant. BMW has already confirmed that the Munich plant will become an EV-only facility, and at first glance, that sounds like good news for the i4. In reality, the Neue Klasse i3 has arrived as the electric 3 Series, and company officials have already said it serves as the de facto replacement. The G26, in both ICE and EV flavors, is expected to bow out early next year.
We aren’t saying BMW has confirmed the i4’s death. It hasn’t. But if the company is looking for models to cut, this one makes a lot of sense.

The iX is another one that looks increasingly difficult to justify. BMW’s original electric flagship SUV launched in 2021, but the company now has a much newer generation of EV technology. The iX5 has already arrived as the fully electric version of the new X5. An electric X7 is also expected to follow in 2027 as the iX7. That leaves the iX between two electric versions of BMW’s established SUV nameplates, both with the Neue Klasse look and tech.
While the iX may already have one foot in the grave, it’ll remain in production throughout 2027. That means customers in some regions might still find one in stock in the first half of 2028. BMW has ended allocations for the United States. Once the iX7 arrives with improvements in a less polarizing package, the case for the iX becomes harder to make.

The XM is the wild card. Unlike the other models on this list, its problem isn’t that BMW already has an obvious replacement waiting in the wings. Its problem is that the business case for another one looks questionable. That doesn’t automatically mean the XM is dead. In fact, we’ve heard that BMW hasn’t completely ruled out a second generation and is exploring ways to address some of the original car’s biggest weaknesses, including its design, packaging, and comfort.
Still, BMW is under pressure to cut low-return models, and the XM isn’t making a compelling argument with its sales numbers. If Munich decides that the future X5 M or X7 M60 is enough of a flagship performance SUV, the XM could end up being a one-generation experiment.

Some of the BMWs on this list don’t need any speculation because they’re already dead. The X4 went out of production in late 2025, while the Z4 and 8 Series bowed out earlier this year.
One important point: BMW may be trimming the lineup, but it’s not simply shrinking. The company is also preparing a wave of new models, including the iX4, which is due to be unveiled very soon. BMW has already announced an i3 Touring, which could see the light of day in 2027.
Earlier this week, BMW confirmed a new entry-level EV for Europe in 2028, widely expected to be the rear-wheel-drive i1 hatchback as the new entry point into its sprawling EV lineup. At the other end of the portfolio, it is evaluating a larger luxury SUV positioned above the X7 for the U.S. market.

What else? An i4 Convertible prototype has already been spotted testing, which strongly suggests BMW is also preparing a two-door i4 Coupe to spread development costs across another model and achieve better economies of scale.
With an i1 on the way, BMW could also do a sedan version as the electric 2 Series Gran Coupe for a higher return on investment. Unlike the hatch, the sedan would have a better chance of coming to the United States. Expect to see the four-door version around the decade’s end.
The iX7 we mentioned earlier should arrive alongside the second-generation X7 next year.
Long story short, BMW is pruning some of the less profitable branches while planting plenty of new ones. As for the on-again-off-again supercar, I’d argue it’s the best and worst time for a spiritual successor to the legendary mid-engined M1. Earlier this year, M CEO Frank said he’d love to work on a modern-day E26.
See more of our coverage in your search results.
